Three-Dimensional Navier–Stokes–Biot Coupling via a Moving Reticular Plate Interface: Existence of Weak Solutions
Felix Brandt, Sunčica Čanić, Boris Muha

Abstract
. We prove the existence of finite-energy weak solutions to a regularized three-dimensional fluid-structure interaction (FSI) problem involving an incompressible, viscous, Newtonian fluid and a multilayered poro(visco)elastic structure. The structure consists of a thick layer modeled by the Biot equations and a thin reticular plate with inertia and elastic energy, transparent to fluid flow. The coupling is nonlinear in the sense that it takes place on a moving interface that is not known a priori but is defined by the solution itself, making the problem a moving-boundary problem. This nonlinear free-boundary coupling, combined with the limited regularity of the Biot displacement, renders the classical weak formulation ill-defined at finite energy. To address this, we introduce a minimally invasive regularization based on a suitable extension and convolution of the Biot displacement, chosen so that the regularized problem remains consistent with the original model. We then construct approximate solutions to the regularized problem via a Lie operator-splitting scheme and derive uniform energy bounds. While these bounds ensure weak and weak* convergence, passing to the limit in the nonlinear terms requires refined compactness arguments, including variants of the Aubin–Lions lemma and tools adapted to moving non-Lipschitz interfaces. The result applies in particular to the purely elastic case (without structural damping) as well as the poroviscoelastic case. This work extends the two-dimensional analysis of [ 30 ] to the fully three-dimensional setting and, to our knowledge, provides the first existence result for a nonlinearly coupled, multilayer 3D Navier–Stokes–Biot FSI system with a permeable interface.



\labelindent

=10pt

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
74F10, 35Q30, 35R35, 74H20

1.
Introduction

Fluid-structure interaction (FSI) problems involving multilayered poroelastic structures naturally arise from a wide range of applications.
We refer here e.g. to encapsulation of bioartificial organs
[
51
]
or blood flow in arteries
[
3
,
13
,
15
]
modeled as poro(visco)elastic media to investigate drug transport through vascular walls.

In this article, we prove the existence of a finite-energy weak solution to a regularized, nonlinearly coupled fluid–poro(visco)elastic structure interaction (FPSI) problem.
More precisely, we study the interaction problem of an incompressible, viscous, Newtonian fluid, represented by the Navier–Stokes equations, as well as bulk poro(visco)elasticity, given by the Biot equations for porous media.
Here, bulk poro(visco)elasticity refers to the fact that the fluid and poro(visco)elastic domains have the same dimension–both are three-dimensional in this work.
We further assume that the free fluid flow and the Biot poro(visco)elastic medium are coupled across a moving interface.
This interface is modeled by a reticular plate with inertia and elastic energy that is transparent to fluid flow.
The geometric setup is sketched in
Figure 1
.

A similar problem, though with two-dimensional fluid and Biot domains and a one-dimensional interface, has been analyzed recently in
[
30
]
.
In the present manuscript, we extend the existence result of a finite-energy weak solution of Leray–Hopf type to the 3D case.
Note that the three-dimensional character of the present problem has a clear meaning in biomedical applications
[
42
]
, and the extension of the result from
[
30
]
to the 3D case requires some non-trivial modifications, as outlined below after the literature review.

The field of FSI involving purely elastic structures located at a part of the fluid boundary has been a very active one in the last 20 years.
The linearly coupled case has been investigated in the situation of linear
[
25
]
and nonlinear models
[
4
,
33
]
.
Strong solutions to FSI problems with the domain of the structure displacement being of a lower dimension than the fluid domain were first studied in
[
5
]
, and the analysis was later extended e.g. in
[
36
,
27
,
38
,
23
,
39
]
.
For investigations of the situation of the fluid and the structure domain being of the same dimension, we refer for example to
[
22
,
18
,
32
,
29
,
46
]
.
The existence of a strong time-periodic solution to an FSI problem with a multilayered structure has been analyzed recently in
[
12
]
.

The study of weak solutions to nonlinearly coupled FSI problems was pioneered in
[
17
]
and further developed e.g. in
[
26
,
41
,
35
]
.
In particular, in
[
41
]
, a constructive approach to FSI problems based on an operator splitting scheme first introduced in
[
28
]
was used to prove the existence of a finite-energy weak solution to an FSI problem between a 1D elastic Koiter shell and a 2D incompressible, viscous, Newtonian fluid.
The underlying idea is to semidiscretize the full nonlinearly coupled problem in time, and to reduce the analysis to fluid and structure subproblems.
This scheme has been successfully applied to different settings including the situation of 3D-2D-FSI problems
[
42
]
, multilayered structures
[
43
]
or elastic shells with nonlinear Koiter energy
[
44
]
.
Our approach likewise employs time discretization combined with Lie operator splitting to construct approximate solutions to the 3D FPSI problem.

Biot
[
7
,
8
]
introduced the equations modeling poroelastic media in the context of soil consolidation, and they are referred to as Biot equations nowadays.
Concerning their mathematical analysis, we mention for instance
[
47
,
10
,
11
]
.
Linearly coupled FSI problems involving poroelastic structures were e.g. considered in
[
48
,
16
,
1
,
6
,
2
]
.
In
[
9
]
, a linearly coupled multilayered FPSI problem of a Stokes flow with a poroelastic plate as well as a poroelastic Biot medium was studied.
The aforementioned work
[
30
]
addresses the nonlinearly coupled FPSI problem of a Navier–Stokes fluid with a multilayered structure consisting of a thin reticular plate and a thick Biot layer in the 2D-1D-setting.

The main mathematical difficulties in the present work stem from the nonlinear coupling of the Navier–Stokes equations with the bulk poro(visco)elasticity.
Due to the same dimension of the thick structural layer and the fluid domain, the regularity of the finite-energy weak solutions is insufficient for defining the moving domain and associated traces. In addition, geometric nonlinearities from the moving domain mean that certain integrals in the weak formulation are not well-defined. To address this, we introduce a minimally invasive regularization: a suitable extension and convolution of the Biot displacement that renders all integrals well-defined.
We then show the existence of a finite-energy weak solution to the emerging regularized FPSI problem.

The next step in this work will be to prove that the regularized problem studied here is consistent with the original problem in the sense that its solution converges to the classical solution of the non-regularized problem when the regularization parameter tends to zero.
Note that the approach from
[
30
]
to prove this type of a weak-classical consistency result cannot be used in the present three-dimensional setting, since the convergence rate of the convolution kernel is not compatible with the loss of regularity following from the odd extension procedure.
In a forthcoming work, a conditional weak-classical consistency result with minimal requirements on the underlying extension and regularization procedure will be provided.

Compared to
[
30
]
, there are additional difficulties arising from the three-dimensional character of the FPSI problem.
In fact, by Sobolev embeddings, the plate displacements in the finite-energy space are no longer Lipschitz continuous, see (
3.2
).
The consequences of this observation are twofold:
First, the fluid domain can no longer be represented as the subgraph of a Lipschitz function, leading to complications in the definition of the solution and test spaces.
As a remedy, we adapt the strategy from
[
42
,
44
]
in the case of 3D FSI problems and invoke the so-called Lagrangian trace, see also
[
17
,
26
,
35
,
40
]
.
Second, we face a loss of regularity when switching between the moving and fixed fluid domain configurations.
This manifests itself in the uniform boundedness estimates of the approximate fluid velocities, providing estimates in Sobolev spaces with lower integrability, which in turn complicates the subsequent compactness arguments.

Here is a summary of the methodology. We prove existence constructively. After specifying the regularization, we time-discretize
[0,T]
into
N
subintervals of length
\Delta t=\frac{T}{N}
and apply Lie splitting to decouple, at each step, a reticular-plate subproblem from the Biot–fluid subproblem. Solving these subproblems sequentially yields the approximate solutions.

We then derive energy estimates uniform in the time step, giving uniform bounds for the approximate solutions.
At this stage, the loss of regularity due to the lower regularity of the plate displacements enters, and it is only possible to show the uniform boundedness of the approximate fluid velocities in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for
p<2
, where
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
denotes the fluid domain that will be made precise in the following section.
Because of the strong coupling, the approaches in
[
17
, Lemma 6]
and
[
43
, Proposition 6.5]
to obtain a Korn equality are not applicable, and the non-Lipschitz geometry rules out the Korn inequality of
[
50
, Lemma 1]
. We therefore use a Korn-type inequality for non-Lipschitz domains
[
34
, Proposition 2.9]
, at the cost of the aforementioned loss of integrability.

The highly nonlinear nature of the present 3D FPSI problem implies that the weak and weak* convergences resulting from the uniform boundedness of the approximate solutions are insufficient for the limit passage.
We invoke suitable compactness criteria to upgrade to strong convergences.
More precisely, we use the classical Aubin–Lions compactness result for the Biot displacement, the Arzela–Ascoli theorem for the plate displacement, the compactness result of Dreher and Jüngel
[
24
]
for piecewise constant functions in the context of the Biot and plate velocity as well as a generalized Aubin–Lions–Simon compactness result due to Muha and Čanić
[
45
]
for the fluid velocity.
The loss of regularity of the plate displacements compared to the 2D case significantly complicates the proof of the compactness of the fluid velocities.

Finally, we pass to the limit in the semidiscretized problem.
Here we first need to show the convergence of the gradients of the fluid velocities, which is due to the loss of regularity compared to the 2D case.
Moreover, we need to construct suitable test functions and discuss their convergence.
The limit passage is based on the above weak, weak* and strong convergences.
We observe that the lack of Lipschitz regularity of the plate displacements requires considerable adjustments in the limit passage.

Note that the local-in-time character of the solution results from the possibility of degeneration of the fluid domain.
To address the maximal time of existence, the procedure from
[
17
, Section 5]
can be adopted to the present setting.
It yields that the maximal time of existence can be extended up to the occurrence of one of the following events:
the moving fluid or Biot domain degenerates, the Lagrangian mapping is no longer injective, or the solution exists globally, i.e.,
T=\infty
.

This work presents the first existence result for a nonlinearly coupled, multilayer 3D Navier-Stokes–Biot fluid–structure interaction (FSI) system with a permeable interface.

The remainder of the article is organized as follows. In
Section 2
, we introduce the multilayered FPSI problem consisting of the Biot subproblem, the reticular plate subproblem, the fluid subproblem as well as suitable coupling and boundary conditions.

Section 3
is dedicated to the introduction of the maps between the reference and physical domains as well as the formal derivation of a weak formulation.
In
Section 4
, we formulate the regularized problem and state the main existence result for a finite-energy weak solution.
In
Section 5
, we present the splitting scheme comprising the plate subproblem as well as the fluid and Biot subproblem.
We also make precise the coupled semidiscrete problem and establish discrete energy equalities.
The construction of the approximate solutions, the proof of their uniform boundedness and the derivation of weak and weak* convergences from there is done in
Section 6
.
In
Section 7
, we perform the compactness arguments for the Biot displacement, the plate displacement, the Biot and plate velocity, the Biot pore pressure and the fluid velocity.

Section 8
is concerned with the limit passage, for which the strong convergence of the fluid velocity traces, the convergence of the test functions on approximate fluid domains and the convergence of the gradients of the fluid velocity are required.
In the final
Section 9
, we provide a summary and give a brief outlook on possible future work.

2.
The fluid-poro(visco)elastic structure interaction problem

We consider a 3D FPSI problem on the time-dependent domain
\Omega(t)=\Omega_{\mathrm{b}}(t)\cup\Gamma(t)\cup\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
(
Figure 1
).
The 3D reference configuration is
\widehat{\Omega}=\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{b}}\cup\widehat{\Gamma}\cup\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
, where

\displaystyle\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{b}}=(0,L)^{2}\times(0,R),\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Gamma}=(0,L)^{2}\times\{0\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}=(0,L)^{2}\times(-R,0).

The mathematical models defined on each of the moving sub-domains are described as follows.

\Gamma(t)
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
\Omega_{(}t)

Figure 1.

A sketch of the
3D
fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
,
3D
Biot domain
\Omega_{(}t)
, and the interface
\Gamma(t)
modeled by the reticular plate equation.

2.1.
The Biot subproblem and the Lagrangian mapping

We begin by introducing the governing equations defined on a fixed domain
\Omega
, originally formulated by M.A. Biot in
[
7
,
8
]
, and then extend this formulation to the case of time-dependent, moving domains.

For a positive time
T>0
, the model variables are the displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}\colon[0,T]\times\Omega_{\to}\mathbb{R}^{3}
of a poroelastic material from its reference configuration
\Omega
and the fluid pore pressure
p\colon[0,T]\times\Omega_{\to}\mathbb{R}
.
The system of coupled PDEs then reads as

(2.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \rho_{\partial}{}_{tt}\boldsymbol{\eta}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{(}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)&=0,&&\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\Omega_{\hfil}\\
c_{0}\partial_{t}p+\alpha(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta})_{t}-\nabla\cdot(\kappa\nabla p)&=0,&&\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\Omega_{\hfil}\end{aligned}\right.

Here,
\rho
is the density of the poro(visco)elastic matrix,
c_{0}
is the storage coefficient,
\alpha
is the Biot-Willis coefficient, and
\kappa
is the permeability of the poro(visco)elastic material, all strictly greater than zero.

In (
2.1
)
1
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{(}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
represents the stress tensor, which is given by

(2.2)

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{(}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{E}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)-\alpha p\boldsymbol{I},

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{E}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
denotes the elastic part. Details about
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{E}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)
will be discussed below.

The first equation (
2.1
)
1
models the elastodynamics of the poroelastic material.
Note that the effect of the pore pressure enters via the term
\alpha p\boldsymbol{I}
in the stress tensor.
On the other hand, (
2.1
)
2
models the conservation of fluid mass.
The term
\kappa\nabla p
in (
2.1
)
2
is related to the filtration velocity
\boldsymbol{q}
via Darcy's law

\boldsymbol{q}=-\kappa\nabla p,\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt\text{$\kappa>0$ constant}.

To study the Biot equations defined on a moving domain, we will consider
the elastodynamics equation (
2.1
)
1
defined in Lagrangian formulation on the reference domain
\widehat{\Omega}
, while (
2.1
)
2
will be considered on the Eulerian domain
\Omega_{(}t)
.
We introduce the following Lagrangian mapping between the two domains

(2.3)

\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{^{\eta}}(t,\cdot)=\Id+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t,\cdot)\colon\widehat{\Omega}_{\to}\Omega_{(}t),

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\colon[0,T]\times\widehat{\Omega}_{\to}\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes the displacement of the Biot poro(visco)elastic matrix, defined on the reference domain. The inverse of the Lagrangian map will be denoted by

(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}(t,\cdot)\colon\Omega_{(}t)\to\widehat{\Omega}
.

Using the Lagrangian mapping we can define the stress tensor (
2.2
), now denoted by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})
, as

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p}):=\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})-\alpha\det\bigl(\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}\bigr)\widehat{p}(\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-\top},

where

\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})=2\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})\boldsymbol{I}+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})\boldsymbol{I}

is the Piola–Kirchhoff stress tensor.
Here
\boldsymbol{D}
represents the symmetric part of the gradient, the notation
\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
indicates that the respective differential operators act on the Lagrangian domain
\widehat{\Omega}
,
\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}
,
\mu_{\mathrm{e}}
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}
,
\mu_{\mathrm{v}}
are Lamé coefficients associated to the elastic and viscoelastic stress, respectively, i.e.,
\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}=\mu_{\mathrm{v}}=0
corresponds to the purely elastic case.

For later reference, we note that on the moving domain, the Piola–Kirchhoff stress tensor can be obtained via the Piola transform as follows:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}_{(}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},p)

\displaystyle=\bigl[\det(\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})(\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{\top}\bigr]\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\det(\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})}\bigl[2\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t})\bigr](\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{\top}\right)\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}.

Recalling the material derivative

\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}+\bigl((\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}(t,\cdot)\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}(t,\cdot))\cdot\nabla\bigr),

we can now express the Biot equations on the moving domain as follows:

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \rho_{\partial}{}_{tt}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}&=\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p}),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\widehat{\Omega}_{\hfil}\\
\frac{c_{0}}{[\det(\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})]\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}p+\alpha\nabla\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\boldsymbol{\eta}&=\nabla\cdot(\kappa\nabla p),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\Omega_{(}t).\end{aligned}\right.

Here, the Biot material displacement
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and the fluid pore pressure
p
on the physical domain
\Omega_{(}t)
in (
2.4
)
2

are given by

\boldsymbol{\eta}(t,\cdot)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\bigl(t,(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}(t,\cdot)\bigr),\kern 5.0ptp(t,\cdot)=\widehat{p}\bigl(t,(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}(t,\cdot)\bigr),\kern 5.0pt\text{where}\kern 5.0pt\Omega_{(}t)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}(t,\widehat{\Omega}_{)}.

At this stage, we observe that there is a subtlety in the definition of the moving domain
\Omega_{(}t)
, namely it is a priori not clear that it is well-defined unless
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
possesses enough regularity, and the map
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}=\Id+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is injective as a map from
\widehat{\Omega}
to
\Omega_{(}t)
.
We shall address this aspect at a later stage, namely in the context of the construction of the approximate solutions in
Section 6
.

2.2.
The reticular plate subproblem

Reticular plates are thin elastic plates with holes/periodic cells distributed in all directions
[
20
]
. The coefficients in the reticular plate equation depend on the periodic structure.
As in
[
9
]
, we restrict ourselves to the transverse plate displacement

\widehat{\omega}\colon[0,T]\times\widehat{\Gamma}\to\mathbb{R}

under the assumption that the stress in the transverse direction dominates over the longitudinal and lateral components. Using
\rho_{\mathrm{p}}>0
to denote reticular plate density, and
\widehat{\Delta}^{2}
for the in-plane bi-Laplacian, the reticular plate equation can be written as

(2.5)

\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\partial_{tt}\widehat{\omega}+\widehat{\Delta}^{2}\widehat{\omega}=\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{p}},\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\widehat{\Gamma},

where
\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{p}}
denotes the source term, and
\widehat{\Gamma}
denotes the reference configuration of the middle surface of the plate. Without loss of generality, we took the coefficient in front of the bi-Laplacian to be equal to 1.

We denote the time-dependent configuration of the plate by

\Gamma(t)=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0<x,\,y<L\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptz=\widehat{\omega}(t,x,y)\}.

It represents the bottom boundary of the moving Biot domain
\Omega_{(}t)
.
We assume that the lateral boundaries and the top boundary of the Biot domain are independent of time,
namely,
\boldsymbol{\eta}=0
on these boundaries.
This also allows us to write the moving Biot domain
\Omega_{(}t)
as

\Omega_{(}t)=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0<x,\,y<L\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\omega}(t,x,y)<z<R\}.

2.3.
The fluid subproblem and ALE mapping

We will be assuming that the fluid occupying
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
is viscous and incompressible, and that the flow is governed by the Navier–Stokes equations

(2.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}&=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{f}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}&=0,&&\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt[0,T]\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t),\end{aligned}\right.

where
\boldsymbol{u}\colon[0,T]\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes the fluid velocity,
and
\pi\colon[0,T]\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\to\mathbb{R}
the pressure.
We further assume the fluid is Newtonian, so that the Cauchy stress tensor takes the form

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{f}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)=2\nu\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u})-\pi\boldsymbol{I},

where
\nu>0
is the kinematic viscosity coefficient.

As in the case of the moving Biot domain, the fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
is not known a priori – it can be expressed in terms of the plate displacement
\widehat{\omega}
as follows:

\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0<x,\,y<L\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt-R<z<\widehat{\omega}(t,x,y)\}.

As we will see below, it is convenient to express the weak formulation of the coupled FPSI problem on a fixed reference domain. To that end, we introduce the following Arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE) mapping

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\colon\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}\to\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
, which maps the reference domain
\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
onto the time-dependent physical domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
:

(2.7)

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})=\Bigl(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z}+\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{z}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\omega}\Bigr),\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})\in\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}.

2.4.
Coupling conditions

The coupling between the Biot subproblem (
2.4
), the reticular plate subproblem (
2.5
) and the fluid subproblem (
2.6
) occurs across the moving reticular plate interface
\Gamma(t)
and is governed by two sets of coupling conditions: kinematic and dynamic.

To state the coupling conditions we introduce the following two sets of notations:

•

The Eulerian structure velocity of the Biot poro(visco)elastic matrix defined at each point of the physical domain
\Omega_{(}t)
:

\boldsymbol{\xi}(t,\cdot)=\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\bigl(t,(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{^{\eta}})^{-1}(t,\cdot)\bigr);

•

Normal unit vectors:
\boldsymbol{n}(t)
denotes the outward unit vector to the boundary of the fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
at
\Gamma(t)
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{n}}
denotes the normal unit vector to the reference fluid domain
\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
at
\widehat{\Gamma}
.

The kinematic coupling conditions.

We assume the following three kinematic coupling conditions at the reticular plate interface:

K1
:

Continuity of normal components of the velocity, or penetration condition, describing conservation of mass of the fluid:

(2.8)

\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t)=(\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t),\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt(0,T)\times\Gamma(t).

K2
:

The Beavers–Joseph–Saffman condition capturing
slip in the tangential component of the fluid velocity:

(2.9)

\beta(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(t)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{f}}\boldsymbol{n}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(t),\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt(0,T)\times\Gamma(t),\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pti=1,2,

where
\beta\geq 0
is a constant, and
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(t)
,
i=1,2
, represent the unit tangent vectors to
\Gamma(t)
.

K3
:

The continuity of displacements:

(2.10)

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3},\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt(0,T)\times\widehat{\Gamma}.

The dynamic coupling conditions.

The following two dynamic coupling conditions are assumed at the reticular plate interface:

D1
:

The interface elastodynamics is governed by Newton's second law of motion, where the net force
\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{p}}
acting on the interface is given by the jump in the normal component of the stress across the interface – with contributions from the fluid on one side and the Biot medium on the other:

(2.11)

\widehat{F}_{\mathrm{p}}=-\det\bigl(\nabla\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\bigr)\bigl[\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{f}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\bigr]\bigl(\nabla\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\bigr)^{-\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{3}+\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{(}\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\right|_{\widehat{\Gamma}},\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt(0,T)\times\widehat{\Gamma}.

D2
:

Continuity of pressure at the interface, i.e., we assume that

(2.12)

-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{f}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\pi)\boldsymbol{n}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(t)+\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}=p,\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt(0,T)\times\Gamma(t).

This condition implies that the reticular plate behaves as a permeable interface, allowing fluid to pass without resistance.

2.5.
The boundary and initial conditions

The FPSI problem studied in this work is supplemented by appropriate boundary and initial conditions.

For the fluid, we assume that the boundary
\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\setminus\Gamma(t)
consists of rigid walls, and thus impose a no-slip condition on the velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}
:

(2.13)

\boldsymbol{u}=0,\quad\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\setminus\Gamma(t).

Similarly, we assume that the boundary of the Biot poro(visco)elastic domain, excluding the interface
\Gamma(t)
, is rigid. Consequently, we enforce no-slip boundary conditions for both the solid displacement
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
and the pore pressure
\widehat{p}
:

(2.14)

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{p}=0,\quad\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\widehat{\Omega}_{\setminus}\widehat{\Gamma}.

The initial conditions for the coupled system are given by:

(2.15)

\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\text{ in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(0),\ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{0},\,\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{0}\text{ in }\widehat{\Omega}_{\ }\widehat{\omega}(0)=\widehat{\omega}_{0},\,\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}(0)=\widehat{\zeta}_{0}\text{ in }\widehat{\Gamma},\ \widehat{p}(0)=\widehat{p}_{0}\text{ in }\widehat{\Omega}

In summary, the 3D FPSI problem under consideration consists of the Biot problem (
2.4
), the reticular plate problem (
2.5
), the fluid problem (
2.6
), the kinematic coupling conditions (
2.8
)–(
2.10
), the dynamic coupling conditions (
2.11
)–(
2.12
), the boundary conditions (
2.13
)–(
2.14
) and the initial conditions (
2.15
).
In the table below, we collect the constants and coefficients appearing in the model described above:

\displaystyle\textbf{Coefficients}

\displaystyle\textbf{Name}

\displaystyle\rho_{\hfil}

\displaystyle\text{Density of poro(visco)elastic matrix;}

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle\text{Storage coefficient;}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\text{Biot-Willis coefficient;}

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle\text{Permeability of the poro(visco)elastic matrix;}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{e}},\lambda_{\mathrm{e}},\mu_{\mathrm{v}},\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle\text{Lam\'{e} constants accounting for elasticity and viscosity;}

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{p}}

\displaystyle\text{Plate density coefficient;}

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\text{Kinematic viscosity coefficient;}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\text{Constant in the context of the Beavers--Joseph--Saffman coupling condition.}

3.
Definition of a weak solution

After formulating the nonlinearly coupled 3D FPSI problem in
Section 2
, we turn to the notion of a weak solution.
Due to the nonlinear coupling, both the fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
and the Biot domain
\Omega_{(}t)
depend on time and are therefore not known a priori in the physical setting. To address this, we introduce mappings from the respective reference domains
\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
,
\widehat{\Gamma}
, and
\widehat{\Omega}
to the corresponding time-dependent physical domains.

At this point, it is important to highlight that the 3D nature of the problem introduces additional analytical difficulties compared to the 2D case treated in
[
30
]
, primarily due to the weaker Sobolev embeddings in three dimensions. As shown in (
3.2
), the plate displacement
\widehat{\omega}
lies in a finite-energy space whose spatial regularity is limited to
\mathrm{C}^{0,r}
for any
r<1
. Consequently, the fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
is, in general, not a Lipschitz domain, and the associated ALE mapping is not necessarily Lipschitz continuous either.

Nevertheless, the
r
-Hölder continuity of
\widehat{\omega}
ensures that
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
can locally be represented as the subgraph of a
\mathrm{C}^{0,r}
function. This regularity is sufficient to define traces on
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
, as established in
[
17
,
26
,
40
]
.

For a treatment of 3D nonlinearly coupled FSI problems without poroelasticity, we refer to the works
[
42
,
44
]
.
We will elaborate more on the difficulties described above in this section as well as in the subsequent section.

3.1.
Maps from the reference to the physical domains

We start by recalling the mappings defined in (
2.3
) and (
2.7
), involving the fluid and Biot domains:

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}(t,\cdot)\colon\widehat{\Omega}_{\to}\Omega_{(}t)\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}(t,\cdot)\colon\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}\to\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t).

Additionally, we introduce the map
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}(t,\cdot)\colon\widehat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma(t)
accounting for the fluid-structure interface, and summarize the three mappings that will be important in this work:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})

\displaystyle=\Id+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z}),

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0pt(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})\in\widehat{\Omega}_{\hfil}

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},0)

\displaystyle=(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{\omega}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})),

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0pt(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})\in\widehat{\Gamma},

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z}+\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{z}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\omega}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0pt(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})\in\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}.

The inverse of the ALE mapping in (
3.1
)
3
is given by

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})^{-1}(x,y,z)=\Bigl(x,y,-R+\frac{R}{R+\widehat{\omega}}(R+z)\Bigr).

Concerning the regularity of the ALE mapping (
3.1
)
3
, we make the following observation.
As also revealed later on, the natural space for the plate displacement
\widehat{\omega}
in the class of finite energy is

\mathcal{V}_{\omega}\coloneqq\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widehat{\Gamma}))\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\widehat{\Gamma})).

From a mixed derivative-type embedding, see
[
21
, Lemma 4]
or also
[
26
, Section 1.2]
, it then follows that

\widehat{\omega}\in\mathcal{V}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}^{\theta,\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{2(1-\theta)}(\widehat{\Gamma})),\kern 5.0pt\text{for all}\kern 5.0pt\theta\in(0,1).

In conjunction with Sobolev embeddings and the fact that
\widehat{\Gamma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the present 3D FPSI problem, the latter embedding leads to

(3.2)

\widehat{\omega}\in\mathcal{V}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{C}^{0,r}(\widehat{\Gamma}))\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0ptr<1.

Because of (
3.2
), the ALE map
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
from (
3.1
)
3
is not necessarily a Lipschitz function.
As we will see below, this also has consequences on the regularity of the fluid velocity
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
defined on the reference domain
\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
.

Next, with regard to the transformation of the associated integrals, we provide the determinants of the Jacobians of the aforementioned maps.
Indeed, straightforward calculations reveal that

(3.3)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{^{\eta}}=\det(\boldsymbol{I}+\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}),\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}=\sqrt{1+|\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\omega}|^{2}+|\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{\omega}|^{2}}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}=1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}.

A few comments on the latter quantities are in order now.
Note that
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}
gives a measure of the arc length difference between the reference and deformed configuration of the plate.
In addition, let us observe that we do not include an absolute value sign in
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
, since we are only concerned with the situation up to degeneracy of the domain, i.e., up to
|\widehat{\omega}|\geq R
.

Regularity of the transformed fluid velocity.

The mappings that we have just made precise allow us to describe the precise way the functions under consideration are transformed.
In particular, the fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
defined on the time-dependent domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
is transformed to the reference domain by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}(t,\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})=\boldsymbol{u}\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega},\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})\in\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}.

With regard to (
3.2
), it does not necessarily follow from
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t))^{3}
that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}})^{3}
.
We shall take this into consideration when making precise the solution and test function spaces in
Subsection 4.1
.

The transformed divergence-free condition.
Recall that the fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
is assumed to be divergence free on the physical domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
, i.e.,
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0
.
The pull-back to the reference configuration generally does not preserve this property, so
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
defined on
\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
is in general not divergence free.
This calls for a reformulation of the divergence free condition on the fixed domain, which we discuss now.
Let
g
be a function defined on
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
.
The definition of the pull-back together with the chain rule then leads to

(3.4)

\nabla g=\nabla\bigl(\widehat{g}\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})^{-1}\bigr)=(\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{g})\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})^{-1}.

Here,
\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
represents the transformed gradient operator, which is of the form

(3.5)

\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\widehat{x}}-(R+z)\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\omega}\frac{R}{(R+\widehat{\omega})^{2}}\partial_{\widehat{z}}\\
\partial_{\widehat{y}}-(R+z)\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{\omega}\frac{R}{(R+\widehat{\omega})^{2}}\partial_{\widehat{z}}\\
\frac{R}{R+\widehat{\omega}}\partial_{\widehat{z}}\end{pmatrix},\kern 5.0pt\text{where}\kern 5.0ptz=\widehat{z}+\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{z}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\omega}.

As a result, the divergence free condition and the symmetric part of the gradient can be rephrased as

\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}})=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}+(\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\top}\Bigr).

The transformed time derivative.
For the transformed time derivative under the map
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
, we obtain

(3.6)

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}-(\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\kern 5.0pt\text{where}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}=\frac{R+\widehat{z}}{R}\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}.

Transformed functions and differential operators on the Biot domain.

We now examine the effect of the transformation
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}
on functions and differential operators defined on the Biot domain.
Let
g
be a scalar function defined on the time-dependent physical domain
\Omega_{(}t)
.
The pull-back of
g
to the fixed reference domain
\widehat{\Omega}
is given by

\widehat{g}=g\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}.

As in the case of the fluid domain, we aim to express differential operators on the physical domain in terms of corresponding operators acting on the reference domain.
In particular,

\nabla g=\nabla(\widehat{g}\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-1})=(\widehat{\nabla}_{^{\eta}}\widehat{g})\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})^{-1},

for
\nabla
representing the gradient on the (time-dependent) physical domain, and
\widehat{\nabla}_{^{\eta}}
denoting a differential operator on the (fixed) reference domain.

Next, we want to calculate the expression for the gradient operator on the reference domain, which we denote by
\widehat{\nabla}
.
Using the chain rule together with the definition of the map
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}
, we obtain

\widehat{\nabla}(g\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}})=[(\nabla g)\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}]\cdot(\boldsymbol{I}+\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}).

Therefore the transformed gradient operator
\widehat{\nabla}_{^{\eta}}
on
\widehat{\Omega}
is of the form

(3.7)

\widehat{\nabla}_{^{\eta}}\widehat{g}=(\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{g},\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{g},\partial_{\widehat{z}}\widehat{g})\cdot(\boldsymbol{I}+\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})^{-1}.

Let us observe that the invertibility of the matrix
\boldsymbol{I}+\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is directly linked to the question of the map

(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})\mapsto(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})

being a bijection between the domains
\widehat{\Omega}
and
\Omega_{(}t)
.

3.2.
Definition of a weak solution

After precisely defining the mappings between the various domains, we now turn to the weak formulation of the 3D nonlinearly coupled FPSI problem under consideration. This formulation will be derived through formal calculations, beginning with the fluid equations.

Before proceeding, we comment on an important issue related to the regularity of the fluid domain and its implications for the trace. In the current setting, the fluid domain can be (locally) represented as the subgraph of a
\mathrm{C}^{0,r}
-function. This allows for the definition of the so-called Lagrangian trace:

(3.8)

\gamma_{\Gamma(t)}\colon\mathrm{C}^{1}\bigl(\overline{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\bigr)\to\mathrm{C}(\Gamma(t)),\kern 5.0pt\gamma_{\Gamma(t)}u\coloneqq u(t,x,y,\widehat{\omega}(t,x,y)).

As established in
[
17
,
26
,
40
]
, this trace operator
\gamma_{\Gamma(t)}
admits a continuous linear extension from
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t))
to
\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma(t))
for
s\in[0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
. The precise result can be found in
[
40
, Theorem 3.1]
.
We note that in the 3D FPSI setting the fluid domain generally fails to be Lipschitz due to the limited regularity of the reticular-plate interface. This distinguishes the 3D, nonlinearly coupled problem from its 2D counterpart. As a result, the weak-solution spaces introduced below for the 3D problem differ from those used in the 2D analysis in
[
30
]
.

Since the formal derivation of the weak formulation is analogous in 2D and 3D, we do not reproduce the standard steps here and instead refer the reader to
[
30
, Section 4.2]
; we also do not delve into the detailed function-space framework or the analytical subtleties caused by the limited regularity of the plate displacement, as these technical aspects are addressed in
Subsection 4.1
. Furthermore, for simplicity, we formulate the weak problem with the fluid velocity defined on the moving domain, thereby avoiding complications related to the potential lack of regularity of the ALE mapping
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
introduced in (
3.1
)
3
. We therefore recall the transverse plate velocity, defined by:

(3.9)

\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}=\widehat{\zeta}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\zeta=\widehat{\zeta}\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega})^{-1}.

Definition 3.1
.

We say that the tuple
(\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},p)
satisfies the weak formulation of the 3D nonlinearly coupled FPSI problem if, for every test function tuple
(\boldsymbol{v},\widehat{\varphi},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},r)
that is
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}
in time on
[0,T)
and satisfy the following conditions:

•

\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0
,
\boldsymbol{v}=0
on
\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\setminus\Gamma(t)
,

•

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0
on
\partial\widehat{\Omega}_{\setminus}\widehat{\Gamma}
,

•

r=0
on
\partial\Omega_{(}t)\setminus\Gamma(t)
,

•

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\widehat{\varphi}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
on
\widehat{\Gamma}
,

the following holds:

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\bigl[((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-2\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}-p\Bigr)(\psi_{\boldsymbol{n}}-v_{\boldsymbol{n}})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\psi_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}}-v_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle-\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\widehat{\Delta}\widehat{\omega}\cdot\widehat{\Delta}\widehat{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}\,\mathrm{d}t-\rho_{\int}{}_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle+2\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}p(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle-c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{r}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\nabla r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}\nabla p\cdot\nabla r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}((\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}(0)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}(0)\cdot\widehat{\varphi}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}+\rho_{\int}{}_{\widehat{\Omega}\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+c_{0}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{p}(0)\cdot\widehat{r}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}.}}

Remark 3.2
.

We emphasize a crucial point: while any classical solution – that is, a solution that is sufficiently smooth in both time and space – of the 3D nonlinear FPSI problem automatically satisfies the weak formulation stated in
Subsection 3.2
, this is not the case for finite-energy weak solutions.

Indeed, if one formally derives an energy estimate following
Subsection 3.2
, one obtains that the following energy estimate holds (at least for classical solutions):

(3.10)

E^{K}(T)+E^{E}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(D_{\mathrm{f}}^{V}(t)+D_{^{V}}(t)+D_{\mathrm{f}^{V}(t)+D_{\beta}^{V}(t)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t=E^{K}(0)+E^{E}(0),}

where

E^{K}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\rho}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}|\widehat{p}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{p}}}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}|\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}}}
,
is the total kinetic energy of the problem,

E^{E}(t)=\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}|\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}|\widehat{\Delta}\widehat{\omega}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}}}

is the elastic energy of the Biot poroelastic matrix and the reticular plate, and

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{f}}^{V}(t)

\displaystyle=2\nu\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},\kern 5.0ptD_{^{V}}(t)=2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}|\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}},}}

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{f}^{V}(t)}

\displaystyle=\kappa\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}|\nabla p|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptD_{\beta}^{V}(t)=\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Gamma(t)}|(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S,

are dissipation terms due to fluid viscosity (
D_{\mathrm{f}}^{V}
), the viscosity of the Biot poro(visco)elastic matrix (
D_{^{V}}
), permeability effects (
D_{\mathrm{f}^{V}}
) and friction in the Beavers–Joseph–Saffman slip condition (
D_{\beta}^{V}
).
Details behind the derivation of this energy equality are presented later in
Subsection 4.2
.

Indeed, this energy equality shows that the structure displacement
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
only belongs to
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)}^{3})
, which is insufficient to ensure that the following integrals in the weak formulation are well-defined:

(3.11)

\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}p(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,\,\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}\frac{\mathrm{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\nabla r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,\,\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t,\,\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{(}t)}\nabla p\cdot\nabla r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The problem arises because the test functions
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
and
\widehat{r}
live in
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)}^{3}
and
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)}
, respectively – on the reference (fixed) domain. Upon transforming to the physical (moving) domain, an additional factor – the Jacobian determinant
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{^{\eta}}=\det(\boldsymbol{I}+\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
from (
3.3
) – must be taken into account. However, this Jacobian is only known to be in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)})
under finite-energy regularity.

As a result, the regularity of
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{^{\eta}}
is not sufficient to guarantee that the integrals in (
3.11
) are finite. This observation is essential: it shows that the weak formulation from
Subsection 3.2
cannot be interpreted in the setting of finite-energy weak solutions. A different, more suitable formulation is therefore required to handle weak solutions in this lower regularity regime.

4.
The regularized problem, notion of a weak solution and existence result

To address the regularity difficulties associated with the Biot problem discussed in
Subsection 3.2
, we introduce a
regularized problem
that serves as an approximation of the original formulation. The regularization differs from the original problem in the smallest number of terms, and is ``consistent'' with the original problem in the sense that, whenever a classical solution to the original problem exists, the solution to the regularized problem converges to it as the regularization parameter tends to zero. A similar regularization strategy was successfully employed in
[
30
]
to overcome weak regularity challenges in the 2D setting.

Since the mathematical difficulties associated with weak regularity stem primarily from the lack of smoothness of the Biot displacement
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
as the Biot domain evolves, we start by
regularizing the Biot displacement
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
on
\widehat{\Omega}
via a convolution with a smooth and compactly supported kernel.
The convolution is chosen carefully, since the domain
\widehat{\Omega}
is bounded – it is defined in a way that preserves the Dirichlet boundary conditions on the lateral boundaries as well as the top boundary.

For this purpose, we introduce an extended domain
\tilde{\Omega}
given by

\tilde{\Omega}_{[}-L,2L]\times[-L,2L]\times[-R,2R].

For
\delta<\min\{L,R\}
, the convolution of a function defined on the extended domain
\tilde{\Omega}
with a smooth and compactly supported function in the closed ball of radius
\delta
yields a function that is well-defined on
\widehat{\Omega}
.

We introduce an odd extension of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
along the lines
\widehat{x}=0
,
\widehat{x}=L
,
\widehat{y}=0
,
\widehat{y}=L
,
\widehat{z}=0
as well as
\widehat{z}=R
.
To this end, let us recall that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
satisfies
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=0
on
\widehat{x}=0
,
\widehat{x}=L
,
\widehat{y}=0
,
\widehat{y}=L
and
\widehat{z}=R
, while it fulfills
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
on
\widehat{z}=0
.
The odd extension of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
to
\tilde{\Omega}
is then given as follows:
On the closure of the original domain – still denoted by
\widehat{\Omega}_{[}0,L]\times[0,L]\times[0,R]
by a slight abuse of notation – we keep the extension – still denoted by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
– the same.
In contrast, outside of the closure of
\widehat{\Omega}
, we successively define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
by

(4.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})&=\omega(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})\boldsymbol{e}_{3}+\bigl(\widehat{\omega}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},-\widehat{z})\bigr),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[0,L]\times[0,L]\times[-R,0],\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})&=-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},2R-\widehat{z}),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[0,L]\times[0,L]\times[R,2R],\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})&=-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(-\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z}),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[-L,0]\times[0,L]\times[-R,2R],\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})&=-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(2L-\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z}),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[L,2L]\times[0,L]\times[-R,2R],\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})&=-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},-\widehat{y},\widehat{z}),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[-L,2L]\times[-L,0]\times[-R,2R],\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y},\widehat{z})&=-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\widehat{x},2L-\widehat{y},\widehat{z}),&&\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt[-L,2L]\times[L,2L]\times[-R,2R].\end{aligned}\right.

Next, let
\sigma
be a radially symmetric function with compact support in the closed unit ball such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=1,\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt

\sigma_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{x})\coloneqq\delta^{-3}\sigma(\delta^{-1}\boldsymbol{x})\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Note that
\sigma_{\delta}
then has compact support in the closed ball of radius
\delta
, and we will use
\sigma_{\delta}
to convolve with the Biot displacement for the regularization.

More precisely, we introduce the following spatially smooth regularized functions on
\widehat{\Omega}
.
First, we define the regularized Biot domain by invoking the odd extension to
\tilde{\Omega}
from (
4.1
) and setting

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}(t,\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}})=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\ast\sigma_{\delta})(t,\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t,\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{y}})\sigma_{\delta}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{y}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{y}},\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Omega}

Accordingly, we define the regularized Lagrangian mapping

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}}(t,\cdot)=\Id+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}(t,\cdot),

as well as the regularized moving Biot domain

\Omega_{^{\delta}}(t)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}}(t,\widehat{\Omega}_{)}.

Let us observe that in spite of the coupling condition
\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right|_{\widehat{\Gamma}}=\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
, we can generally not guarantee that
\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\right|_{\widehat{\Gamma}}=\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
.
Therefore, we also define the regularized moving interface

\Gamma^{\delta}(t)=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}}(t,\widehat{\Gamma}).

An alternative interpretation is that
\widehat{\Gamma}^{\delta}
is the plate interface when displaced from the reference configuration
\widehat{\Gamma}
in the direction
\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\right|_{\widehat{\Gamma}}
.
Note that this is a purely transverse displacement.

Another important observation is that by the odd extension described in (
4.1
), we have

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\partial\widehat{\Omega}_{\setminus}\widehat{\Gamma}.

We can now introduce a
regularized weak formulation
of the
3D
nonlinearly coupled FPSI with regularization parameter
\delta
.
First, we introduce the solution and test space.
These are motivated by the subsequent energy estimates.
After that, we state the regularized weak formulation in the moving domain framework.

4.1.
Weak solutions to the regularized problem

In this subsection, we define a weak solution to the
regularized 3D nonlinearly coupled FPSI problem
.
We emphasize that, as already touched upon in
Section 3
, the transition from the 2D setting studied in
[
30
]
to the present 3D framework introduces several additional mathematical challenges – most notably due to the lower spatial regularity of the plate displacement
\widehat{\omega}
.

Specifically, the regularity result in (
3.2
) necessitates a more refined construction of the solution and test spaces, including an extension of the Lagrangian trace operator from (
3.8
) to accommodate reduced spatial regularity.
Furthermore, the lack of Lipschitz continuity of the ALE map in 3D complicates the transformation between the reference and physical domains, affecting the formulation of the fluid equations.
Our approach builds upon and generalizes techniques developed in
[
42
,
44
]
to handle the unique analytical obstacles posed by the 3D setting.

In the definition below, we present the solution and test spaces, taking into account the reduced regularity of the plate displacement and the associated difficulties with the trace as well as the ALE mapping.

Definition 4.1

(Solution and test spaces)
.

(1)

For the fluid part on the moving domain, i.e., in the Eulerian formulation, we first define

\prescript{}{0}{V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\coloneqq\bigl\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}\bigl(\overline{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\bigr)^{3}:\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{u}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0ptx,y\in\{0,L\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptz=-R\bigr\}.

Considering the discussion at the beginning of
Subsection 3.2
, we set

V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\coloneqq\overline{\prescript{}{0}{V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}}^{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t))^{3}}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{f}}\coloneqq\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t))^{3})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)).

Similarly as in
[
17
,
26
]
, one can verify that
V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
can be characterized as

V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\coloneqq\bigl\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t))^{3}:\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{u}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0ptx,y\in\{0,L\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptz=-R\bigr\}.

(2)

With regard to the fact that the ALE mapping
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
from (
3.1
)
3
is in general not Lipschitz continuous, implying that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
is not necessarily in
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}})^{3}
, we set

(4.2)

V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\coloneqq\bigl\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}:\boldsymbol{u}\in V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\bigr\}.

The associated fluid part of the finite-energy solution space is then defined by

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\coloneqq\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}).

(3)

Concerning the plate subproblem, we introduce the function space

\mathcal{V}_{\omega}\coloneqq\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widehat{\Gamma}))\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\widehat{\Gamma})).

(4)

In the context of the Biot displacement, we invoke

(4.3)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{d}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)}^{3}:\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{x},\widehat{y}\in\{0,L\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{z}=R\kern 5.0pt\text{as well as}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\eta}_{x}=\widehat{\eta}_{y}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Gamma}\bigr\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{&}\coloneqq\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)}^{3})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;V_{\mathrm{d}})\cap\mathrm{H}^{1}(0,T;V_{\mathrm{d}}).

(5)

For the Biot pore pressure, we define

(4.4)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{p}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl\{\widehat{p}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)}:\widehat{p}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{x},\widehat{y}\in\{0,L\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{z}=R\bigr\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{&}\coloneqq\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widehat{\Omega}_{)})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;V_{\mathrm{p}}).

(6)

The weak solution space with the fluid velocity defined on the moving domain is given by

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{sol}}\coloneqq\bigl\{(\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{f}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\omega}\times\mathcal{V}_{\times}\mathcal{Q}_{:}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Gamma}\bigr\}.

(7)

The weak solution space with the fluid velocity defined on the fixed domain takes the form

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{sol}}^{\omega}\coloneqq\bigl\{(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\times\mathcal{V}_{\omega}\times\mathcal{V}_{\times}\mathcal{Q}_{:}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Gamma}\bigr\}.

(8)

We define the test space for the fluid on the moving domain by

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{test}}\coloneqq\bigl\{(\boldsymbol{v},\widehat{\varphi},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\widehat{r})\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}([0,T);V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)\times\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\widehat{\Gamma})\times V_{\mathrm{d}}\times V_{\mathrm{p}}):\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\widehat{\varphi}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Gamma}\bigr\}.

(9)

The test space for the fluid defined on the fixed domain is given by

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{test}}^{\omega}\coloneqq\bigl\{(\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}},\widehat{\varphi},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\widehat{r})\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}([0,T);V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\times\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\widehat{\Gamma})\times V_{\mathrm{d}}\times V_{\mathrm{p}}):\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\widehat{\varphi}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\Gamma}\bigr\}.

Below, we comment on the Hilbert space structure of the space
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
introduced in
Subsection 4.1
(b).

Remark 4.2
.

Under the non-degeneracy assumption
|\widehat{\omega}|<R
on the domain, we can define a scalar product on
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
as follows:

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}})_{V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}}\coloneqq\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}:\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}},

where
\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
is the transformed gradient defined in (
3.5
).
Recalling the Jacobian
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}=1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}
from (
3.3
) and exploiting the definition of
\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
, we find that

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}})_{V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}}=\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}+\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}:\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}=\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t))^{3}}.

As a result,
\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
is an isometric isomorphism between
V_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
and
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
, rendering
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
a Hilbert space.

After introducing the appropriate solution and test spaces in
Subsection 4.1
, we now discuss the weak formulation of the regularized problem involving the moving fluid domain.

Definition 4.3

(Weak formulation of the regularized problem with moving fluid domain)
.

A tuple
(\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{p})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{sol}}
is a weak solution to the regularized 3D nonlinearly coupled FPSI problem with regularization parameter
\delta
if for any test function
(\boldsymbol{v},\widehat{\varphi},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\widehat{r})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{test}}
, the following weak formulation is satisfied:

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\bigl[((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-2\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}-p\Bigr)(\psi_{\boldsymbol{n}}-v_{\boldsymbol{n}})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\psi_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}}-v_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle-\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\widehat{\Delta}\widehat{\omega}\cdot\widehat{\Delta}\widehat{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}\,\mathrm{d}t-\rho_{\int}{}_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle+2\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}):\widehat{\boldsymbol{D}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}})(\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{^{\delta}}(t)}p(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle-c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{p}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{r}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{^{\delta}}(t)}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\delta}}{\mathrm{D}t}\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\nabla r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma^{\delta}(t)}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\delta})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{^{\delta}}(t)}\nabla p\cdot\nabla r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}((\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(0)}\boldsymbol{u}(0)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}(0)\cdot\widehat{\varphi}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}+\rho_{\int}{}_{\widehat{\Omega}\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+c_{0}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{p}(0)\cdot\widehat{r}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}},}}

where the material derivative with respect to the regularized displacement is given by

\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\delta}}{\mathrm{D}t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}+(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}\cdot\nabla),\kern 5.0pt\text{with}\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\delta}(t,\cdot)=\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\delta}\bigl(t,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}})^{-1}(t,\cdot)\bigr),

the vector
\boldsymbol{n}
represents the upward pointing normal vector to
\Gamma(t)
, and
\boldsymbol{n}^{\delta}
is the upward pointing normal vector to
\Gamma^{\delta}(t)
.

As mentioned earlier, we introduced a minimal regularization, modifying only the four terms in the weak formulation that require it, while keeping all others unchanged, as in
[
30
]
.

Remark 4.4
.

Notation.

To ease notation, we will use the following conventions throughout the rest of this work:

(1)

Even though the solution to the above regularized problem depends on the regularization parameter
\delta
implicitly, we will omit writing
\delta
explicitly if it is clear from the context that we are dealing with the solution to the regularized problem.

(2)

We also omit writing the compositions with the maps
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta}}
as well as
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}}
and their inverses explicitly.
As a result, we simply write

-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{^{\delta}}(t)}p\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\kern 5.0pt\text{instead of}\kern 5.0pt-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{^{\delta}}(t)}\Bigl(\widehat{p}\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}})^{-1}\Bigr)\nabla\cdot\Bigl(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}})^{-1}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

or

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}((\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t\kern 5.0pt\text{instead of}\kern 5.0pt-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}((\boldsymbol{u}-(\zeta\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega})^{-1})\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\widehat{r}\circ(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega})^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t.

We note that the above considerations implicitly assume the invertibility of the map
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{^{\eta^{\delta}}}
. This assumption will be addressed in more detail later, as it leads to a constraint on the time interval of existence. Specifically, we will show that invertibility can be ensured on a time interval
[0,T_{\delta}]
, where the length of the interval
T_{\delta}
may depend on the regularization parameter
\delta
.

Next, we present the weak formulation of the regularized problem with the fluid velocity defined on the fixed domain. To this end, recall that the Jacobians
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{^{\eta}}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
, as defined in (
3.3
), naturally arise in the transformation from the moving domains to the corresponding fixed reference domains.

We start with the first term in
Subsection 4.1
.
Using of the non-degeneracy assumption
|\widehat{\omega}|<R
, invoking the transformed time derivative from (
3.6
), also recalling
\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}=\frac{R+\widehat{z}}{R}\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
, employing the transformed fluid gradient from (
3.5
), and integrating by parts in the
z
-direction, we find that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}(t)}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\bigl[(\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\frac{1}{R}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\bigl[(R+\widehat{z})\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\partial_{\widehat{z}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\frac{1}{2R}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\bigl[(R+\widehat{z})\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\partial_{\widehat{z}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2R}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}(\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega})\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}+\frac{1}{2R}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\bigl[(R+\widehat{z})\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\partial_{\widehat{z}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr]\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}})\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\partial_{t}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\widehat{\omega}}{R}\Bigr)\bigl[((\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}-((\widehat{\boldsymbol{w}}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2R}\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}}(\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega})\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}})\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}.

Using the expression for the normal vector to the interface
\boldsymbol{n}=(-\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\omega},-\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{\omega},1)/\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}
, as well as the interface condition
\left.\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\right|_{\Gamma(t)}=\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
, which is a result of (
2.10
), we find that

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widehat{\Gamma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}})\partial_{t}\widehat{\omega}\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{S}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Therefore, the last term in (
4.5
) can be combined with one of the terms from
Subsection 4.1
.

Remark 4.5
.

Notation for scaled normal and tangent vectors.

Since the factor
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}
in the unit normal vector cancels out with the Jacobian of the transformation from
\Gamma(t)
to
\widehat{\Gamma}
, we introduce the following normal and tangent vectors:

(4.6)

\widehat{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\omega}=(-\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\omega},-\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{\omega},1),\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}^{\omega}=(1,0,\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\omega})\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{\widehat{x}}\widehat{\omega}|^{2}}}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\widehat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}^{\omega}=(0,1,\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{\omega})\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{\widehat{y}}\widehat{\omega}|^{2}}}.
