The proof of this result is different from the 2D case because of the lower regularity of the plate displacement, and the weaker result on the uniform boundedness of the fluid velocity in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T,\mathrm{W}^{1,p})
instead of
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T,\mathrm{H}^{1})
for the 2D case.

The main idea is to use a compactness criterion for piecewise constant functions due to Dreher and Jüngel
[
24
]
.
We introduce the Biot and plate velocity test space
\mathcal{Q}_{v}
, which encodes slightly higher regularity in the Biot component compared to
Subsection 4.1
, and which is given by

\mathcal{Q}_{v}\coloneqq\{(\boldsymbol{\psi},\varphi)\in(V_{\mathrm{d}}\cap\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})\times\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\Gamma):\boldsymbol{\psi}=\varphi\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\Gamma\}.

For
s\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
, it then follows that
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3}\times\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)\Subset\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{)}\times\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime}
.
Let us stress that the first embedding is compact, and this chain of embeddings will be required for the compactness criterion
[
24
]
.

Moreover, we recall the time shift
\tau_{\Delta t}
given by
\tau_{\Delta t}f(t,\cdot)=f(t-\Delta t,\cdot)
for a function
f
defined on
(0,T)
.
The Dreher–Jüngel compactness criterion still requires us to show that for
C>0
uniform in
N
, we have

(7.1)

\left\|\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N},\zeta_{N})-(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N},\zeta_{N})}{\Delta t}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\Delta t,T;\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime})}+\|(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N},\zeta_{N})\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}\times\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))}\leq C.

From (
6.2
), it follows that
\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}(t)=\frac{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}(t)
and
\partial_{t}\overline{\omega}_{N}(t)=\zeta_{N}(t)
.
Therefore, the uniform boundedness of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
is a consequence of the uniform boundedness of
\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}
in
\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
as shown in
Section 6
, while the uniform boundedness of
\zeta_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))
follows in the same way from the uniform boundedness result of
\overline{\omega}_{N}
in
Section 6
.
This implies that the second term in (
7.1
) is uniformly bounded.

For the first term, we invoke the coupled semidiscrete formulation from (
5.4
) and (
5.5
), where we choose the test functions
\boldsymbol{v}
for the fluid velocity and
r
for the Biot pore pressure equal to zero.
Thus, upon replacing
\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}
by
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}
, and using that
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}=\zeta_{N}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
on
\Gamma
, for
(\boldsymbol{\psi},\varphi)\in\mathcal{Q}_{v}
, we obtain

(7.2)

\displaystyle\rho_{\int}{}_{{\Omega}\Bigl(\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\int_{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta_{N}^{n+1}-\zeta_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\Bigr)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}S}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}-p_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega_{N}^{n}})\,\mathrm{d}S-\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}(\zeta_{N}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\omega_{N}^{n}})\,\mathrm{d}S

\displaystyle-2\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{{\Omega}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n+1}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}-\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n+1})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}-2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{{\Omega}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n+1}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}}}}

\displaystyle-\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n+1})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\alpha\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}p_{N}^{n+1}\nabla_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\omega_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Delta\varphi\,\mathrm{d}S.}}

By construction, the estimate of the first term in (
7.1
) is implied provided we manage to estimate the right-hand side in (
7.2
) by the
\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime}
-norm.
Consequently, let
(\boldsymbol{\psi},\varphi)\in\mathcal{Q}_{v}
such that
\|(\boldsymbol{\psi},\varphi)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{v}}\leq 1
.
In particular, it follows that
\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Omega_{)}}\leq 1
and
\|\varphi\|_{\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq 1
.
Now, let us observe that most of the terms on the right-hand side in (
7.2
) can be controlled easily when taking into account the uniform boundedness results established in
Section 6
and the underlying uniform energy estimates.
In particular, let us observe that similar uniform boundedness results for
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}
as for
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}
can be derived in the viscoelastic case
\mu_{\mathrm{v}}
,
\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}>0
upon recalling the shape of the discrete dissipation
D_{N}^{n+1}
from
Subsection 5.3
.
We will only make precise the treatment of a few selected terms to show the general ideas.
Due to the uniform boundedness of
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
, choosing
p>\frac{3}{2}
and making use of classical properties of the trace and Sobolev embeddings, we find that
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}
admits a uniform bound in
\mathrm{W}^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\Gamma)^{3}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{q}(\Gamma)^{3}
for some
q>2
.
Likewise, employing the uniform boundedness of
p_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)})
, we derive a uniform bound of
p_{N}^{n}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)
.
On the other hand, for
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3}
, we get
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathrm{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Gamma)^{3}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)^{3}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)^{3}
.
Note that
\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
can be bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{r}(\Gamma)
by definition of
\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
as made precise in (
4.6
), the uniform boundedness of
\omega_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\Gamma))
and the Sobolev embedding
\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}^{1,r}(\Gamma)
for all
r\in(1,\infty)
.
Putting together these arguments, and using Hölder's inequality, for all
n\in\{0,1,\dots,N-1\}
, we find

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}-p_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega_{N}^{n}})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)}+\|p_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\bigr)\leq C,

where
C>0
is independent of
N
by the above arguments.
The other term on the right-hand side in (
7.2
) that we discuss in detail is the term with the factor
\alpha
.
For this, we recall
\mathcal{J}_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}=\det(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta})
and
\nabla^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}=\tr\bigl[\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta})^{-1}\bigr]
.
From Assumption 2.C and
\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)}}\leq 1
, we deduce the uniform boundedness of
\|\nabla^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}}
.
On the other hand, the boundedness of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n}
in
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)}^{3}
as discussed above yields the uniform boundedness of the Jacobian involving the regularized Biot displacement, i.e.,
|\mathcal{J}_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}|\leq C
for
C>0
independent of
N
.
Consequently, additionally invoking the uniform boundedness of
p_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)})
, we get
\left|\alpha\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}p_{N}^{n+1}\nabla_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C}
.
Putting the various estimates together, we infer the existence of a constant
C>0
independent of
n
and
N
such that

\left\|\frac{(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n+1},\zeta_{N}^{n+1})-(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n},\zeta_{N}^{n})}{\Delta t}\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime}}\leq C.

It then follows that

\left\|\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N},\zeta_{N})-(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N},\zeta_{N})}{\Delta t}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(\Delta t,T;\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime})}\leq(\Delta t)\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\left\|\frac{(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n+1},\zeta_{N}^{n+1})-(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}^{n},\zeta_{N}^{n})}{\Delta t}\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{v}^{\prime}}\leq CT.

We have now shown that all the assumptions of
the compactness criterion of Dreher and Jüngel
[
24
]
are satisfied, which implies the compactness result of
Subsection 7.3
.
∎

7.4.
Compactness for the Biot pore pressure

In this subsection, we show the compactness result for the pore pressure
p_{N}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

There is a subsequence
p_{N}
so that
p_{N}\to p
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)})
.

The proof of this result relies on the compactness criterion of Dreher and Jüngel
[
24
]
for piecewise constant functions and follows the same reasoning as the proof of the corresponding 2D result, Theorem 8.2 in
[
30
]
.

7.5.
Compactness for the fluid velocity

To prove compactness of the fluid velocity we employ a generalized Aubin–Lions compactness theorem for functions on moving domains introduced in
[
45
]
.
To use the generalized Aubin–Lions compactness result from
[
45
]
we first transform the fluid problem back onto the physical domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq x,y\leq L,\,-R\leq z\leq\omega_{N}^{n}(x,y)\}
with the associated moving boundary

\Gamma_{N}^{n}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq x,y\leq L,\,z=\omega_{N}^{n}(x,y)\}.

We slightly redefine the solution and test spaces for the fluid velocity as follows:

(7.3)

\displaystyle V_{N}^{n+1}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}:\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n},\,\boldsymbol{u}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{on}\kern 5.0pt\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}\setminus\Gamma_{N}^{n}\bigr\},

\displaystyle Q_{N}^{n}

\displaystyle=V_{N}^{n+1}\cap\mathrm{H}^{3}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}\cap\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}.

Note that
\mathrm{H}^{3}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})
, so the intersection with
\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}
does not affect the space.
In particular, when equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\coloneqq\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{H}^{3}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}+\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
, we observe that the space
Q_{N}^{n}
is isomorphic to the Hilbert space
\mathrm{H}^{3}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}
.
The reason why we intersect the space
V_{N}^{n+1}
with
\mathrm{H}^{3}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}\cap\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})^{3}
is to simplify the upcoming estimates.
It is important to note that this more restrictive space is dense in the
natural test space.

For the semidiscrete formulation of the approximate fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\in V_{N}^{n+1}
, we recall the coupled semidiscrete formulation (
5.4
) and set the test functions
\varphi
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and
r
equal to zero.
Moreover, we transform the integrals onto the physical domain, invoke the relation of the
z
-component on the fixed and the moving domain given by
\frac{\widehat{z}+R}{R}=\frac{R+z}{R+\omega}
, where we denote by
\widehat{z}
the
z
-component on the fixed domain, and introduce
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}=\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\circ\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n-1}}\circ(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}})^{-1}
for the ALE map
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}\colon\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}\to\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}
as made precise in
Subsection 3.1
, to address the mismatch of domains for the plate displacement to get that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in Q_{N}^{n}

(7.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+2\nu\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle-\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{v}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2R}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{R}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}S

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-p_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\,\mathrm{d}S-\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\,\mathrm{d}S=0.

To be able to compare the functions on different physical domains,
we introduce a maximal domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
containing all the physical domains:

\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq x,y\leq L,\,-R\leq z\leq M(x,y)\},

where
M(x,y)
is specified below in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exist smooth functions
m(x,y)
and
M(x,y)
with
m=M=0
on
\partial\Gamma
such that
m(x,y)\leq\omega_{N}^{n}(x,y)\leq M(x,y)
for all
x,y\in[0,L]
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
n=0,1,\dots,N
.
Moreover, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
n=0,1,\dots,N-1
and
l=0,1,\dots,N-n
as well as
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there are
\alpha
-Hölder-continuous functions
m_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)
and
M_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)
whose Hölder constants can be bounded above by some constant
L>0
that is independent of
n
,
l
and
N
with

(1)

m_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)\leq\omega_{N}^{n+i}(x,y)\leq M_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)
for all
x
,
y\in[0,L]
and
i=0,1,\dots,l
,

(2)

M_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)-m_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)\leq C(l\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{4}}
, for all
x
,
y\in[0,L]
,

(3)

\|M_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)-m_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C(l\Delta t)
,

where the constant
C>0
is independent of
n
,
l
and
N
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement on the existence of suitable functions
m
and
M
is a consequence of
Section 6
.

For the second part of the statement
we fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and consider finitely many functions
\omega_{N}^{n+i}(x,y)
for
i=0,\dots,l
defined on the time interval from
t=n\Delta t
to
t+h=n\Delta t+l\Delta t
.
We then define
m_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)
and
M_{N}^{n,l}(x,y)
,
x
,
y\in[0,L]
, to be the functions obtained by considering the minimum and maximum of the finitely functions
\omega_{N}^{n+i}(x,y)
for
i=0,\dots,l
.
If necessary, we mollify the functions to get sufficient regularity.
This shows the validity of (i).

To show (ii), we proceed as in the proof of
Subsection 7.2
, and find that the upper bound on the norm
\|\omega_{N}^{n+l}-\omega_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Gamma)}
only depends on the length of the interval, namely for
C>0
independent of
N
,
n
and
l
, to get
\|\omega_{N}^{n+l}-\omega_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Gamma)}\leq Ch^{\frac{1}{4}}
.
Thanks to the embedding
\mathrm{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}(\Gamma)
, we obtain (ii).
Finally, (iii) is a consequence of the uniform energy bound for the plate velocity in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))
as revealed in
Section 6
.
∎

Once the maximal domain is defined, we extend the fluid velocities

\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}
from
\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}
to the common maximal domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
by zero, so that we can compare the velocities defined on the maximal domain.
However, as the plate displacements
\omega_{N}^{n}(x,y)
are merely
\alpha
-Hölder-continuous for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, it is a priori not clear that such an extension by zero is possible.
At this stage, we invoke
[
31
, Theorem 8.2]
, see also
[
49
, Corollary B.2]
for a similar result:

Lemma 7.6
.

Suppose that
\omega\colon\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous with
\sup_{\Gamma}|\omega|\leq M
.
Let
\Omega_{\omega}
be the subgraph defined by
\Omega_{\omega}\coloneqq\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(x,y)\in\Gamma\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt0<z<\omega(x,y)\}
.
Moreover, for
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\omega})
, define the extension by zero by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt\Omega_{\omega}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{in}\kern 5.0pt\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Omega_{\omega}
.
Then for all
s<\frac{\alpha}{2}
, we find that

\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\omega})},

where the constant
C>0
only depends on
s
,
M
and
\alpha
.

Thanks to
Subsection 7.5
, we may extend the fluid velocities by zero and deduce the uniform boundedness.

Lemma 7.7
.

The approximate fluid velocities that are obtained by extension by zero, and which we still denote by
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
, are uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3})
for
s\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.

Proof.

Let
s\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
be arbitrary.
There exist
s^{\prime}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
s^{\prime}\alpha=s
.
Using Korn-type inequality from
[
34
, Proposition 2.9]
,
similarly as in
Section 6
, we find that
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
.
By Sobolev embeddings, there exists
p\in(\frac{6}{5},2)
such that
\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{s^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
.
From
Subsection 7.5
together with the uniform boundedness of
\omega_{N}^{n}
implied by
Section 6
, there exists a constant independent of
N
and
n
, such that the extension by zero
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}
satisfies

\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}(t)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}

for
t\in(n\Delta t,(n+1)\Delta t]
.
By the above uniform boundedness result, we conclude the assertion.
∎

We are now in the position to discuss the relative compactness of the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
.

Proposition 7.8
.

The sequence
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
obtained from an extension by zero to the maximal domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
is relatively compact in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3})
.

The proof is based on the generalized Aubin–Lions compactness lemma
[
45
]
.
For completeness, we state the theorem in the variant required for the present setting in
Appendix A
.

Proof of
Subsection 7.5
.

We start by showing that all the assumptions of Theorem 3.1 in
[
45
]
as stated in
Appendix A
hold.

First, we introduce the overarching Hilbert spaces:

H=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptV=\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3}\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt0<s<\frac{1}{2}.

From
Subsection 7.5
, the maximal domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
can be chosen to be Lipschitz continuous, so by the compactness of Sobolev embeddings, we obtain that
V\Subset H
.
Next, we define the spaces
V_{N}^{n}
and
Q_{N}^{n}
in the statement of Theorem 3.1 in
[
45
]
to be the spaces (
7.3
) above.
As required by Theorem 3.1 in
[
45
]
,
(V_{N}^{n},Q_{N}^{n})\hookrightarrow V\times V
where the continuous embedding can be achieved using the extension by zero to the maximal domain, uniformly in
n
and
N
.
Furthermore, the chain of embeddings
V_{N}^{n}\Subset\overline{Q_{N}^{n}}^{H}\hookrightarrow(Q_{N}^{n})^{\prime}
readily follows by construction.
It now remains to check the validity of (A1), (A2), (B), (C1), (C2) and (C3) from Theorem 3.1 in
[
45
]
.

Verification of Property(A):
The uniform boundedness properties in (A) follow from
Subsection 7.5
, uniform energy estimates (
6.3
), and
Section 6
.

Verification of Property(B):

We start by considering the semidiscrete formulation for the fluid velocity on the physical domain from (
7.4
)
with
\boldsymbol{v}\in Q_{N}^{n}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\leq 1
, and derive that

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\nu\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{v}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|+\frac{1}{2R}\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{R}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|+\left|\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-p_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|

\displaystyle+\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|.

Proving Property(B) entails showing that the right-hand side is bounded uniformly in
N
,
n
and
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}
.
First, it readily follows that

2\nu\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}

by definition of the space
Q_{N}^{n}
as well as
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\leq 1
.

The estimates of the following terms are more involved, which is due to the lack of Lipschitz continuity of the plate displacement.
For the next term, we first use Hölder's inequality and make use of the boundedness of the factor
\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}
to get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bigl(\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}+\|\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\bigr)\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}.

Now, we discuss the terms on the right-hand side of the above inequality.
To this end, we recall that the Jacobian of the ALE map
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
given by
1+\frac{\omega_{N}^{n}}{R}
is uniformly bounded thanks to the uniform boundedness of the family of plate displacements.
In conjunction with the uniform energy estimate of the fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}
on the fixed domain, this yields that
\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
can be bounded by a constant that is independent of
N
and
n
.
On the other hand, for
\|\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}
, we also invoke the uniform energy estimates as presented in the proof of
Section 6
.
Thus, it remains to estimate
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
.
For this purpose, we make use of the uniform boundedness of the Jacobian of the ALE map, use the Sobolev embedding
\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
for
p\in(3,4)
and employ
[
35
, Lemma 2.6]
to find an estimate of the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}
-norm by the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
-norm, where the constant merely depends on the uniform bound of the plate displacements instead of
N
and
n
.
Note that we will not distinguish variables on moving and fixed domains in terms of notation, but the distinction will become clear from the context.
Thus, still denoting the transformed version of
\boldsymbol{v}
on
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
by
\boldsymbol{v}
, we get

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\leq C

by definition of the space
Q_{N}^{n}
.
Putting together these arguments, we find that

\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}},

where the constant
C>0
is uniform in
N
and
n
.
For the other term that is associated with the convective term, we use Hölder's inequality, handle the terms related to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}
and
\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
as above, invoke the definition of the space
Q_{N}^{n}
, and employ the uniform boundedness of the Jacobian of the ALE map together with the embedding
\mathrm{W}^{1,r}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
for
r\in(\frac{12}{7},2)
and
[
35
, Lemma 2.6]
for the estimate of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,r}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}
by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
with a constant that is uniform in
n
and
N
.
This strategy leads to

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{R+z}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\Bigr)\boldsymbol{v}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bigl(\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}+\|\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\bigr)\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\cdot\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,r}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}.

For the next term, we may proceed similarly as above:
We estimate
\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)
, estimate
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
by
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}
and handle
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}
in
\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})
as we have seen in the preceding estimate to get

\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{R}{R+\omega_{N}^{n}}\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C\cdot\|\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}

for a constant
C>0
that is uniform in
N
and
n
.

The estimates of the boundary integrals require some further preparation.
In particular, we stress that due to the lack of Lipschitz regularity of the plate displacements, the considerations are significantly different from the 2D case.
First, let us recall the Jacobian of the transformation from the interface
\Gamma_{N}^{n}
to the fixed interface
\Gamma
.
It is given by

\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}=\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}^{n}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}^{n}|^{2}}.

At this stage, we invoke the uniform boundedness of the family
\omega_{N}^{n}
in
\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\Gamma)
, and as in the proof of
Section 6
, we note that
\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}^{1,s}(\Gamma)
for all
s<\infty
.
Hence, for every
s<\infty
, we can estimate
\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
in
\mathrm{L}^{s}(\Gamma)
by a constant that is uniform in
N
and
n
.
Next, we discuss how to estimate
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
.
In this regard, we observe that
\mathrm{W}^{\frac{5}{7},\frac{7}{2}}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)
by Sobolev embeddings.
In addition, using continuity of the trace from
\mathrm{W}^{1,\frac{7}{2}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
to
\mathrm{W}^{\frac{5}{7},\frac{7}{2}}(\Gamma)
,
[
35
, Lemma 2.6]
in order to estimate
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,\frac{7}{2}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}
by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}
, with a constant that is independent of
N
and
n
, as well as the definition of the space
Q_{N}^{n}
, we find that

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{\frac{5}{7},\frac{7}{2}}(\Gamma)}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,\frac{7}{2}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\leq C.

Let us emphasize that the (generic) constant
C>0
is independent of
N
and
n
.
By the relations shown in (
6.2
) as well as the uniform energy estimates established in the context of
Section 6
, we infer that
\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}
can be uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)
.
It remains to handle the norms involving
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}
.
When choosing
s<\infty
in
\mathrm{L}^{s}(\Gamma)
sufficiently large for the above handling of the Jacobian
\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
, we may consider
\mathrm{L}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\Gamma)
.
For
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, it then follows that
\mathrm{W}^{\frac{4}{9}-2\varepsilon,\frac{9}{5}}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\Gamma)
.
Additionally making use of classical properties of the trace and the embedding
\mathrm{H}^{1-\varepsilon,\frac{9}{5}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
, complex interpolation, the uniform boundedness of
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
as revealed in
Section 6
, and
[
35
, Lemma 2.6]
in order to control the transformation back to the moving domain, we get

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{\frac{4}{9}-2\varepsilon,\frac{9}{5}}(\Gamma)}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1-2\varepsilon,\frac{9}{5}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1-\varepsilon,\frac{9}{5}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{9}{5}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,\frac{9}{5}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}^{1-\varepsilon}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n})}^{1-\varepsilon}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}^{1-\varepsilon}.

Upon noting that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}
have the same trace along the interface
\Gamma
, we argue that the term involving
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}
can be handled in a similar way.
Concatenating the previous estimates, and exploiting the elementary estimate
1+(ab)^{\delta}\leq(1+a+b)^{2\delta},\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pta,b\geq 0\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\delta\in(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},1)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bigl(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\Gamma)}+\|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\bigr)\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{5}{2}}(\Gamma)}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(1+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}^{1-\varepsilon}\bigr)\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}^{1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bigl(1+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}\bigr)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}.

We stress that the (generic) constant
C>0
above is independent of
N
and
n
, and
2(1-\varepsilon)<2
is valid.
Similarly, we find that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-p_{N}^{n+1}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bigl(1+\|p_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}\bigr)^{2(1-\varepsilon)},\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt

\displaystyle\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{N}^{n}}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\,\mathrm{d}S\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\bigl(1+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}\bigr),

where
C>0
is again uniform in
N
and
n
.
Combining these estimates, we find that

\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n}}\leq 1}\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C\cdot\bigl(a_{N}^{n}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}\bigr)^{2(1-\varepsilon)},

where
C>0
is uniform in
N
and
n
, and
a_{N}^{n}\coloneqq 1+\|p_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)}}
is such that

(\Delta t)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}|a_{N}^{n}|^{2}\leq 2\bigl((\Delta t)N+\|p_{N}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)})}^{2}\bigr)\leq C.

The boundedness of the pore pressure follows from
Section 6
.
By (
7.5
), the above estimates lead to

(7.6)

\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C\cdot\bigl(a_{N}^{n}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}\bigr)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}

for
C>0
uniform in
N
and
n
and
\varepsilon>0
small so that
2(1-\varepsilon)\in(1,2)
.

To finish proving that Property(B) from Theorem 3.1 in
[
45
]
holds, it remains to estimate the term
\bigl|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\bigr|
.
Indeed, following the approach in
[
45
, Theorem 4.2]
, see also
[
14
, Theorem 10.6]
for the 3D case, we get the existence of a constant
C>0
that is uniform in
N
and
n
such that

(7.7)

\left|\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}.

Combining (
7.6
) and (
7.7
), we find that there exists a constant
C>0
that is independent of
N
and
n
,
a_{N}^{n}\coloneqq 1+\|p_{N}^{n+1}\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)}}
with
(\Delta t)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}|a_{N}^{n}|^{2}\leq C
and
p\in[1,2)
such that

\left\|P_{N}^{n}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\right\|_{(Q_{N}^{n})^{\prime}}\leq C\bigl(a_{N}^{n}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}\bigr)^{p}

for all
n=0,1,\dots,N-1
.
This completes the verification of Property(B).

Verification of Property(C):
This property is crucial for compactness on moving domains as it specifies the conditions under which one can take time derivatives of functions defined on different domains.
Conditions (C1) and (C2) introduce ``common'' test and solutions spaces that depend smoothly on the time shifts, locally in time.
Condition (C3) is a ``global'' estimate in which one has to show that Conditions (C1) and (C2) hold uniformly for all
n
,
N
, and time-shifts by
l\Delta t
.

The proof of
Property(C1)
can be obtained analogously to the respective proof in
[
45
, Theorem 4.2]
upon defining the common test space
Q_{N}^{n,l}
similarly as
Q_{N}^{n}
in (
7.3
) and estimating the test function
\boldsymbol{v}
carefully to guarantee uniformity of the constants in
N
,
l
and
n
.

Property(C2)
follows from a
squeezing procedure
in the wake of
[
45
, Definition 4.2]
.
Concerning the last estimate required for (C2), we get

\|I_{N,l,n}^{i}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}\bigl(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M_{N}^{n,l}}\bigr)}\leq C\bigl((l\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{4}}+(l\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{8}}\bigr)\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V_{N}^{n+i}},

completing the proof for the function
g(h)\coloneqq C\bigl(h^{\frac{1}{4}}+h^{\frac{1}{8}}\bigr)
.

The proof of the uniform Ehrling property,
Property(C3)
, is similar to the one in
[
45
, Section 4.2]
, so we omit the details here.

Now that all the assumptions of Theorem 3.1 in
[
45
]
as formulated in
Appendix A
have been verified, the compactness results for fluid velocity stated in
Subsection 7.5
follows by applying this theorem.
∎

8.
Passing to the limit in the regularized weak formulation

In this section, we pass to the limit in the regularized weak formulation in order to show existence of a finite-energy weak solution as stated in
Theorem 4.8
.
For this purpose, we summarize the strong convergences that we have established so far.

A summary of strong convergence results:

(1)

\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{\eta}
in
\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
, see
Subsection 7.1
.

(2)

\overline{\omega}_{N}\to\omega
in
\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
and
\omega_{N}\to\omega
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
for
s\in(0,2)
, see
Subsection 7.2
.

(3)

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{\xi}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
and
\zeta_{N}\to\zeta
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
for
s\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
, see
Subsection 7.3
.

(4)

p_{N}\to p
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)})
, see
Subsection 7.4
.

(5)

\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3})
, see
Subsection 7.5
.

(6)

\zeta_{N}^{*}\to\zeta
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
for
s\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
.

Concerning the last statement, it might not be clear that
(\zeta_{N})
and
(\zeta_{N}^{*})
converge to the same limit in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
,
however, (
6.4
) implies

\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\|\zeta_{N}^{n+\frac{1}{2}}-\zeta_{N}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\leq C,\kern 5.0pt\text{so}\kern 5.0pt\|\zeta_{N}-\zeta_{N}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))}\to 0.

We will use these convergence results to pass to the limit in the semidiscrete coupled formulation (
5.4
).
Before passing to the limit, we need some further preparation.
First, we identify the weak limit of the family of the gradients of the fluid velocity.
Second, we establish the strong convergence for the traces of the fluid velocity on the boundary of the fluid domain.
Third, we show a convergence result for the test functions defined on approximate domains.
These steps will be carried out in the following subsections.

8.1.
Convergence of the gradient of the fluid velocity

From
Section 6
, we recall that for all
p\in(1,2)
, the sequence
(\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N})
converges weakly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
to some
\boldsymbol{G}
.
Here, we identify this weak limit as the gradient of the limit of the sequence of fluid velocities
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
.
More precisely, we have the following.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
,
\boldsymbol{G}
and
\omega
be the weak* limit of the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
, the weak limit of the sequence
(\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N})
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
and the weak* limit of the sequence
\omega_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}_{0}^{2}(\Gamma))
, respectively.
Then it holds that
\boldsymbol{G}=\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\boldsymbol{u}
.

The proof of this result is conceptually similar to the proof of Proposition 7.6 in
[
43
]
, so we omit the details here for brevity.

8.2.
Strong convergence of the fluid velocity traces at the interface
\Gamma

Recall the definition of the time shift
\tau_{\Delta t}
introduced in
Section 6
. The main result in this subsection is the following.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}=\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\circ\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
.
Then the traces
\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}\right|_{\Gamma}
of the approximate fluid velocities on
\Gamma
converge to the trace of the limiting fluid velocity on
\Gamma
as
N\to\infty
, i.e., for
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, we have
\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}\right|_{\Gamma}\to\left.\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|_{\Gamma}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma)^{3})
.

The proof of
Subsection 8.2
will be carried out in several steps.
One important ingredient is the lemma below on strong convergences.
Let us emphasize that even though the
assumptions are weaker
than in the 2D case, see
[
30
, Lemma 9.1]
, we do get convergence in the same spaces as in 2D.

Lemma 8.3
.

Assume that
(\boldsymbol{f}_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{f}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
as well as
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{f}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
Then for
s\in(0,1)
, it follows that
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{f}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
Moreover, we have
\left.\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\right|_{\Gamma}\to\left.\boldsymbol{f}\right|_{\Gamma}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma)^{3})
for
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

Proof.

Let
s\in(0,1)
.
For some
p\in(1,2)
, we find
s^{\prime}\in(s,1)
with
s^{\prime}-\frac{3}{p}>s-\frac{3}{2}
, so
\mathrm{H}^{s^{\prime},p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
by Sobolev embeddings.
Furthermore, by complex interpolation, we have
\mathrm{H}^{s^{\prime},p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})=[\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}),\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})]_{s^{\prime}}
.
Thus, additionally using that
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})
for
p\in(1,2)
, we find that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\|(\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f})(t)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\cdot\int_{0}^{T}\|(\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f})(t)\|_{\mathrm{H}^{s^{\prime},p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\int_{0}^{T}\|(\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f})(t)\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}^{2(1-s^{\prime})}\cdot\|(\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f})(t)\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})}^{2s^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}^{2(1-s^{\prime})}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}^{2s^{\prime}}.

Now, the factor
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}
is uniformly bounded by assumption, while for the first factor, we observe that it tends to zero as
N\to\infty
thanks to the assumption on the strong convergence of
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{f}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
On the other hand, by classical properties of the trace, for
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, we get

\|\left.\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\right|_{\Gamma}-\left.\boldsymbol{f}\right|_{\Gamma}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma))}^{2}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{f}_{n}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}^{2}.\qed

We are now in the position to prove
Subsection 8.2
.

Proof of
Subsection 8.2
.

The main idea is to link the strong convergence
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3})
from
Subsection 7.5
with the uniform boundedness of
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
and its limit
\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
as discussed in
Section 6
, and to employ
Subsection 8.2
.

In the first part of the proof, we shall verify that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}\to\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
.
To this end, we express the relevant difference
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}^{2}
in terms of
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
and
\boldsymbol{u}
defined on the maximal fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
.
In fact, we get

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\left|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr)-\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega\Bigr)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\left|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr)-\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\left|\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr)-\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega\Bigr)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\eqqcolon I_{1}+I_{2}.

In order to handle the integral
I_{1}
, we invoke the uniform boundedness of the term
1+\frac{\omega_{N}^{n}}{R}
to find that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{n\Delta t}^{(n+1)\Delta t}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega_{N}^{n}}{R}\Bigr)|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{n\Delta t}^{(n+1)\Delta t}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}))}^{2}.

Thus, with regard to the strong convergence
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3})
by
Subsection 7.5
, the integral
I_{1}
indeed tends to zero as
N\to\infty
.

Concerning the second integral,
I_{2}
, we introduce

z^{*}(t,x,y)=\frac{R(\omega(t,x,y)-\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}(t,x,y))}{R+\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}(t,x,y)}.

Thus, for
z^{*}
, it holds that
z^{*}+(1+\frac{z^{*}}{R})\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}=\omega
.
With the above
z^{*}
, we further split
I_{2}
, namely,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=I_{2,1}+I_{2,2},\kern 5.0pt\text{where}\kern 5.0pt

\displaystyle I_{2,1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\int_{-R}^{\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))}\left|\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr)-\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega\Bigr)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle I_{2,2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))}^{0}\left|\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t,

where for
I_{2,2}
, we used that
\boldsymbol{u}
is extended by zero to the maximal fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
, so it vanishes for values of
z
that exceed
\omega
.
For the estimate of
I_{2,1}
, the fundamental theorem of calculus implies that

\int_{z+(1+\frac{z}{R})\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}^{z+(1+\frac{z}{R})\omega}\partial_{z^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{u}(t,x,y,z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega\Bigr)-\boldsymbol{u}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\Bigr).

Consequently, using Hölder's inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{2,1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\int_{-R}^{\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))}\left(\int_{z+(1+\frac{z}{R})\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}(t,x,y)}^{z+(1+\frac{z}{R})\omega(t,x,y)}|\partial_{z^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{u}(t,x,y,z^{\prime})|\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\right)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\int_{-R}^{\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))}\left|\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\cdot\bigl(\omega(t,x,y)-\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\bigr)\right|^{\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{z+(1+\frac{z}{R})\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}(t,x,y)}^{z+(1+\frac{z}{R})\omega(t,x,y)}|\partial_{z^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{u}(t,x,y,z^{\prime})|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t.

In a similar way as in the proof of
Section 6
, one can show that the term
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}(t)))}
is uniformly bounded.
On the other hand, as shown in
Subsection 7.2
and also recalled at the beginning of this section, for
s\in(0,2)
, we have
\overline{\omega}_{N}\to\omega
strongly in
\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
.
By virtue of (
6.11
), this yields the pointwise uniform convergence of
\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}\to\omega
in
[0,T]\times\Gamma
as
N\to\infty
.
Putting together these observations, we conclude that
I_{2,1}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

Next, we tackle
I_{2,2}
.
For this, we use the embedding
\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(-R,\omega)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(-R,\omega)
for all
p>1
to obtain

\displaystyle I_{2,2}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}|\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))|\cdot\max_{z\in[-R,\omega(t,x,y)]}|\boldsymbol{u}(t,x,y,z)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}|\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))|\cdot\int_{-R}^{\omega(t,x,y)}|\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{u}(t,x,y,z)|^{p}+|\boldsymbol{u}(t,x,y,z)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t.

The convergence
I_{2,2}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
then follows from
|\min(0,z^{*}(t,x,y))|\to 0
uniformly on
[0,T]\times\Gamma
, which is based on the aforementioned convergence of
\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}
to
\omega
, together with the uniform boundedness of
\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})^{3})
as well as
\nabla\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})^{3\times 3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
.
Therefore, we have shown that
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})}\to 0
.

Next, we show that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
so that we can use
Subsection 8.2
to obtain the final convergence result.
To this end, we recall from
Section 6
that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
.
At the same time,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
is the strong limit of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
and thus also in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for
p\in(1,2)
.
Therefore, as
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}
converges weakly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
along a subsequence to a weak limit that has to coincide with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
, we argue that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}
is also in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.

We can now combine the previous steps and use
Subsection 8.2
to obtain the strong convergence result of fluid velocity traces as stated in
Subsection 8.2
.
∎

8.3.
Convergence of the test functions on approximate fluid domains

One obstacle in passing to the limit is that the test functions for the fluid velocity in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{test}}^{\omega}
, see
Subsection 4.1
, are defined on the fixed reference domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
and satisfy
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0
on
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
, while the test functions in the semidiscrete formulation lie in the test space
Q_{N}^{n}
and satisfy
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0
in
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
.
Thus, we need to find a way to compare the test functions in
Q_{N}^{n}
to those in the test space
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{test}}^{\omega}
.
The idea is to use test functions that are defined on the maximal domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
, and which are constructed such that the restrictions of these test functions to the domains associated with
\omega
and composed with the ALE mapping
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
lead to a space of test functions
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
that is dense in the fluid velocity test space
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
.
We then denote the space of all such test functions defined on
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
by
\mathcal{X}
.
More precisely, due to the reduced regularity of the ALE mapping, we need to define the test space in a slightly more careful way.
First, we introduce

\prescript{}{0}{V_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}}\coloneqq\bigl\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}})^{3}:\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{v}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0ptx,y\in\{0,L\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptz=-R\bigr\}.

We then define
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}\coloneqq\overline{\prescript{}{0}{V_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}}}^{\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3}}
.
Similarly as mentioned in the context of
Subsection 4.1
, one can verify that
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
admits the characterization

V_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}=\bigl\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3}:\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{v}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0ptx,y\in\{0,L\}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptz=-R\bigr\}.

The space
\mathcal{X}
consists of functions
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}([0,T);\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3})
such that for all
t\in[0,T)
, the following hold:

(1)

\boldsymbol{v}(t)
is a smooth vector-valued function in
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
,

(2)

\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0
in
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
,

(3)

\boldsymbol{v}(t)=0
on
\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}\setminus\Gamma_{M}
, where
\Gamma_{M}=\{(x,y,M(x,y)):0\leq x,y\leq L\}
is the top boundary of the maximal fluid domain
\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M}
.

For
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{X}
, we set

(8.1)

\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\coloneqq\left.\boldsymbol{v}\right|_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\coloneqq\left.\boldsymbol{v}\right|_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}}.

Let us observe that the test functions
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
are dense in the fluid velocity test space
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}
related to the fixed domain formulation, while the test functions
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
restricted to the time intervals
[n\Delta t,(n+1)\Delta t)
are dense in
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
, with
V_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}
as made precise in (
4.2
).
Thus, for every fixed
N
, we can take into account the semidiscrete formulation with the test function
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
.
Note that the
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
are discontinuous in time due to the jumps in
\omega_{N}
at each time step
n\Delta t
.

In the lemma below, we state the convergence result for the test functions
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
and their gradients.
We emphasize that the lack of Lipschitz continuity implies that we cannot obtain uniform convergence of the gradients as in the 2D case. Instead, the gradients converge only in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}^{M})^{3\times 3}))
for all
p<\infty
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
as well as
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
be as defined in (
8.1
).
Then
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
pointwise uniformly in
[0,T]\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
and
\nabla\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\nabla\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3\times 3}))
for all
p<\infty
.

Proof.

The proof is inspired by the one of
[
41
, Lemma 4]
.
In fact, let us first recall that

\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(t,x,y,z)=\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega(t,x,y)\Bigr)\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}(t,x,y,z)=\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\Bigr).

Thus, making use of the mean value theorem, we find that

\displaystyle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}(t,x,y,z)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(t,x,y,z)|

\displaystyle=\left|\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\Bigr)-\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega(t,x,y)\Bigr)\right|

\displaystyle=|\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{v}(t,x,y,\zeta)|\cdot\left|\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)(\omega_{N}(t,x,y)-\omega(t,x,y))\right|.

From here, as
(1+\frac{z}{R})
is clearly bounded, and invoking the boundedness of
|\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{v}(t,x,y,\zeta)|
thanks to the smoothness assumption on
\boldsymbol{v}
coming from the test space as well as the uniform convergence of
\omega_{N}
to
\omega
by
Subsection 7.2
and the Sobolev embedding
\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma)
for
s>1
, we deduce the first convergence statement of the lemma.

For the second part, for
i\in\{x,y\}
, we compute
by the chain rule that

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}(t,x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\Bigl[\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\Bigr)\Bigr]=\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\partial_{i}\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\cdot\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega_{N}(t,x,y)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(t,x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega(t,x,y)\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\partial_{i}\omega(t,x,y)\cdot\partial_{z}\boldsymbol{v}\Bigl(t,x,y,z+\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{R}\Bigr)\omega(t,x,y)\Bigr).

The desired convergence of
\partial_{i}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}(t,x,y,z)
to
\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{v}
then follows in a similar way as the uniform convergence in the first part of the proof upon additionally invoking the uniform boundedness of
\partial_{i}\omega_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
for all
p<\infty
and the convergence of
\partial_{i}\omega_{N}
to
\partial_{i}\omega
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
for all
p<\infty
thanks to
Subsection 7.2
.
The procedure for controlling the difference
\partial_{z}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}-\partial_{z}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
is similar, finishing the proof of this lemma.
∎

The construction of the above test space
\mathcal{X}
is considerably simpler than the corresponding construction in the situation of FSI problems with purely elastic structures, where the fluid and and structure test spaces are related via a kinematic coupling condition, included in the velocity test space.
This is not the case for fluid-poroelastic structure interaction problems and the fluid test space in our case can therefore be decoupled from the rest.

8.4.
Passage to the limit in the regularized weak formulation

Here, we pass to the limit in the semidiscretized formulation of the regularized problem in order to prove the main result,
Theorem 4.8
.
To this end, we take a test function
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N},\varphi,\boldsymbol{\psi},r)
for given
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{X}
, where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
was introduced in (
8.1
).
For every
n=0,1,\dots,N-1
, we then test the semidiscrete formulation (
5.4
) with
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}(t),\varphi(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t),r(t))
for every
t\in[n\Delta t,(n+1)\Delta t)
and integrate in time from
n\Delta t
to
(n+1)\Delta t
and then sum from
n=0
to
N-1
to obtain an integral over
[0,T]
.
By definition of the approximate solutions as made precise in
Section 6
, for each
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N},\varphi,\boldsymbol{\psi},r)
in the test space with
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{X}
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+2\nu\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{D}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-p_{N}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}

\displaystyle-\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2R}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\zeta_{N}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\rho_{\int}{}_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\Bigl(\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}-\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}}{\Delta t}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\rho_{\mathrm{p}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{t}\overline{\zeta}_{N}\cdot\varphi\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+2\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}):\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}p_{N}\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle+c_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\partial_{t}\overline{p}_{N}\cdot r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}\cdot\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}}

\displaystyle-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}(\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}p_{N}\cdot\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}r\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}((\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}})r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\omega_{N}\cdot\Delta\varphi\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Since many terms are analogous to those in classical fluid-structure interaction problems (see, e.g.,
[
41
, Section 7.2]
), we will focus only on the terms that are specific to the poroelastic structure interaction problem or that involve the regularized displacement.

Let us start with a term involving the Jacobian of the regularized deformation gradient. Note that regularized terms will converge in arbitrary Sobolev spaces in the spatial variable, and thus are easy to handle.
More precisely, since the regularization does not affect the time variable, using
Section 6
, we conclude that
(\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N})^{\delta}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
.
On the other hand, by definition of the regularization, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\|\nabla^{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N})^{\delta}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)})}\leq C
, for a constant
C=C(\delta)
that is uniform in
N
.
Hence, in a similar way as in
Subsection 7.1
, we deduce from the Aubin–Lions compactness lemma that there exists a strongly convergent subsequence, still denoted by
(\overline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N})^{\delta}
, that converges to
\boldsymbol{\eta}
strongly in
\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{C}^{k}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Similarly to the plate displacement, by invoking the Lipschitz continuity in time and proceeding as in the proof of
Section 6
, we further argue that this convergence carries over to
(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}
.
In particular, we conclude that

\mathcal{J}_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}=\det\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}\bigr)\to\det\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}\bigr)=\mathcal{J}_{^{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}}}

strongly in
\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{C}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
.
The aforementioned convergence also implies that

\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}\bigr)^{-1}\to\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}\bigr)^{-1}=\nabla_{^{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}

strongly in
\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathrm{C}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
, and similarly for
\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}r\to\nabla_{^{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}}}r
.
Thus, from the strong convergence of
p_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)})
by
Subsection 7.4
, the weak* convergence of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
, which follows from
Section 6
and the relation between
\boldsymbol{\xi}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, as well as the weak convergence of
p_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}p_{N}\nabla_{^{(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}}}p\nabla_{^{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\delta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.}

The desired convergence of the other three integrals involving the regularized Biot displacement follows likewise upon invoking the weak* convergence of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{)}^{3})
, which follows from
Section 6
and the relation of
\boldsymbol{\xi}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, the weak convergence of
p_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega_{)})
as well as the weak* convergence of
\zeta_{N}^{*}
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))
following from
Section 6
.

Next, we proceed with the term
\frac{1}{2R}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\zeta_{N}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t
.
To this end, let us note that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\zeta_{N}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\partial_{t}\omega\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|\leq\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}(\zeta_{N}-\partial_{t}\omega)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\partial_{t}\omega(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|+\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\partial_{t}\omega\boldsymbol{u}\cdot(\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|.

For the first term, we recall from (
6.2
) and
Section 6
that
\zeta_{N}\rightharpoonup^{*}\partial_{t}\omega
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))
.
Moreover, we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))
.
Indeed, we recall
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
In
Subsection 8.3
, we have verified the uniform convergence
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
.
Concerning the second term, by virtue of the strong convergence of
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
, it remains to argue that
\partial_{t}\omega\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
In fact, from the above convergences, we infer that
\partial_{t}\omega\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))
, while
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
, yielding that
\partial_{t}\omega\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
As a result the second term also tends to zero as
N\to\infty
.
For the last term, we invoke the uniform convergence
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
as well as the fact that
\partial_{t}\omega\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}))
and
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
Finally, we conclude that

\frac{1}{2R}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\zeta_{N}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\frac{1}{2R}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\partial_{t}\omega\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Most remaining terms include the normal or tangential vectors
\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}=(-\partial_{x}\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N},-\partial_{y}\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N},1)
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}=(1,0,\partial_{x}\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N})
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}=(0,1,\partial_{y}\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N})
, so we elaborate on their handling in the following.
In fact, the strong convergence of
\omega_{N}\to\omega
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
for
s\in(0,2)
, which also carries over to
\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}
, together with the embedding
\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Gamma)
for all
p<\infty
for
s\in(0,2)
sufficiently close to
2
implies that
\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\to\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\to\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\omega}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Gamma))
for all
p<\infty
.
From
Subsection 8.3
and the embedding
\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C}(\overline{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}})
for
p>3
, we deduce that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
uniformly on
(0,T)\times\overline{\Omega}_{\mathrm{f}}
, so in particular, the uniform convergence holds on the interface
\Gamma
. To handle the quadratic term in the fluid velocity, we use
Subsection 8.2
to conclude that
\left.\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\right|_{\Gamma}\to\left.\boldsymbol{u}\right|_{\Gamma}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma)^{3})
for
s\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, and therefore, by Sobolev embedding for
s
sufficiently close to
\frac{1}{2}
, we have
\left.\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\right|_{\Gamma}\to\left.\boldsymbol{u}\right|_{\Gamma}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{3}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{3}(\Gamma)^{3})
. Moreover, the same argument applies to
\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
. An analogous result holds for the fluid pore pressure
p_{N}
, and therefore we conclude:

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-p_{N}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}-p\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t.

With regard to the term
\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t
, we can proceed similarly for the factors
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
,
\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}
,
\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
, while we additionally make use of the weak* convergence of
\zeta_{N}^{*}
to
\zeta
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Gamma))
to argue that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega}-\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega})\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t.

In the same way, we find that

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\bigl((\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\bigr)r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\bigl((\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\omega}\bigr)r\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t.

The treatment of the remaining terms is more delicate.
Let us start with the term associated to the Beavers–Joseph–Saffman condition, namely
\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t
.
The difficulty in the limit passage for this term arises from
\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}
and the factors in the tangential vectors.
For the first term involving
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}
, we need to handle

\frac{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}}}{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}}.

As the consideration of the second term is analogous, we focus on the first one.
Therefore, we set
f_{N}\coloneqq\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}}
,
f\coloneqq\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}}
,
g_{N}\coloneqq 1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}
and
g\coloneqq 1+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}
.
As
g
and
g_{N}
are bounded from below by
1
, it follows that

(8.2)

\left|\frac{f_{N}}{g_{N}}-\frac{f}{g}\right|=\left|\frac{(f_{N}-f)g}{g_{N}g}\right|+|f|\cdot\left|\frac{g-g_{N}}{g_{N}g}\right|\leq|f_{N}-f|+|f|\cdot|g-g_{N}|.

Next, we employ the elementary identity
