\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b}=\frac{a-b}{\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}}
for
a
,
b>0
to get

\displaystyle f_{N}-f

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}}-\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}-|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}-|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}}+\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}}}.

Now, exploiting that the denominator of the preceding fraction is bounded below by two, we argue that

|f_{N}-f|\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}-|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}-|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}\bigr).

The strong convergence of
\omega_{N}
now implies that
f_{N}\to f
in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Gamma))
for all
p<\infty
.
Concerning the second term in (
8.2
), we proceed analogously as above, namely we expand it as

\displaystyle g-g_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}-|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}-|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}-|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}}+\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}}}.

Again, we note that the denominator can be bounded from below by two, while for the numerator, we use that

|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}-|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}=\bigl(|\partial_{x}\omega|^{2}-|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}\bigr)\cdot|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}+|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}\cdot\bigl(|\partial_{y}\omega|^{2}-|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}\bigr),

and the terms
|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}
and and the terms
|\partial_{y}\omega_{N}|^{2}
and
|\partial_{x}\omega_{N}|^{2}
can be controlled again in
\mathrm{L}^{r}(\Gamma)
for large
r
.
Similarly, we find that
|f|
can be bounded in
\mathrm{L}^{r}(\Gamma)
for large
r
.
In total, with regard to (
8.2
), we conclude that the difference under consideration converges strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{r}(\Gamma))
for large
r\in(1,\infty)
.
Putting together these arguments, we derive the convergence

\displaystyle\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}cJ_{\Gamma}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega}(\zeta\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\omega}(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\omega}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t.

Finally, we discuss the treatment of the convective terms, and we treat the four terms separately.
Notice that the main difference from the classical FSI problems is that now the plate velocity strongly converges only in negative Sobolev spaces, since there is no direct dissipation acting on the plate.
First observe that
(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R})\to(1+\frac{\omega}{R})
uniformly on
(0,T)\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
.
Let us start with
(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}})\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
.
From
Subsection 8.3
, we recall the uniform convergence of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
in
(0,T)\times\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}
.
On the other hand, applying
Subsection 8.2
to the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
as in the proof of
Subsection 8.2
, we argue that
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for
s\in(0,1)
.
Thus, by Sobolev embeddings, it follows in particular that
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
Note that this convergence carries over to
\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
.
Moreover, we invoke from
Subsection 8.1
the weak convergence of
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
to
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
.
A concatenation of these arguments leads to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega}{R}\Bigr)\bigl((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

In a similar way, this time using that
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3\times 3})
for all
p^{\prime}<\infty
by
Subsection 8.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega}{R}\Bigr)\bigl((\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Next, we take into account the term
(\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}})\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}
.
Let us recall that
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\rightharpoonup\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\boldsymbol{u}
weakly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3\times 3})
for all
p\in(1,2)
, while
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,{p^{\prime}}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
p^{\prime}<\infty
.
On the other hand, we recall from
Subsection 7.3
that
\zeta_{N}\to\zeta
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
for
s\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
.
For
s^{\prime}\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
and
q>2
, we then derive the strong convergence of
\zeta_{N}\to\zeta
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{s^{\prime},q}(\Gamma))
for some
q>2
.
Thus, recalling that
\boldsymbol{w}=\frac{R+z}{R}\partial_{t}\omega\boldsymbol{e}_{3}
we deduce the convergence

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega}{R}\Bigr)\bigl((\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The final term includes
(\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}})\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
.
Here we note that
\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\to\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3\times 3})
for all
p<\infty
by
Subsection 8.3
.
Moreover, we recall that
\zeta_{N}
converges strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s}(\Gamma))
for
s\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)
, while as in the proof of
Subsection 8.2
, we argue that
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{s^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for all
s^{\prime}<1
.
From there, we deduce the existence of
s^{\prime\prime}\in(0,s^{\prime})
and
q>2
such that
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{s^{\prime\prime},q}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
.
In total, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega}{R}\Bigr)\bigl((\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega})\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

By combining the last four convergence results, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}

\displaystyle-\Bigl(\Bigl(\Bigl(\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\zeta_{N}\frac{R+z}{R}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\tau_{\Delta t}\omega_{N}}\Bigr)\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_{N}\Bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega}{R}\Bigr)\Bigl[\bigl(\bigl((\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w})\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\bigr)\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}-\bigl(\bigl((\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w})\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega}\bigr)\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

This proves the first statement of
Theorem 4.8
.

In the last part of the proof we derive an energy estimate for the weak solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},p,\omega)
resulting from the limit passage in the splitting scheme as described above.
For this purpose, let us recall from (
6.3
) the estimate

E_{N}^{n+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}D_{N}^{j+1}\leq E_{0}

for all
n=0,\dots,N-1
, where

\displaystyle E_{N}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega_{N}^{n}}{R}\Bigr)\bigl|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\rho}{2}\int_{{\Omega}\bigl|\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{p}}}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\bigl|\zeta_{N}^{n+1}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\int_{{\Omega}\bigl|p_{N}^{n+1}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n+1})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}}{2}\int_{{\Omega}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n+1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\omega_{N}^{n+1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S}}}

and

\displaystyle D_{N}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2\nu(\Delta t)\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\omega_{N}^{n}}{R}\Bigr)\bigl|\boldsymbol{D}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1})\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}(\Delta t)\int_{{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{D}(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}(\Delta t)\int_{{\Omega}|\nabla\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\kappa(\Delta t)\int_{{\Omega}\mathcal{J}_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}\bigl|\nabla_{^{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}^{n})^{\delta}}}p_{N}^{n+1}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}}}

\displaystyle+\beta(\Delta t)\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\mathcal{J}_{\Gamma}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}\bigl|(\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{\omega_{N}^{n}}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S.

Hence, employing the definition of the approximate solutions from
Section 6
, invoking the relations of
\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}
as well as
\dot{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{N}
and
\zeta_{N}^{*}
on the interface
\Gamma
, and transforming to the respective moving domains, for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, we deduce the energy inequality

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}(t)}\bigl|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\rho}{2}\int_{{\Omega}\bigl|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\int_{{\Omega}\bigl|p_{N}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\mu_{\mathrm{e}}\int_{{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{e}}}{2}\int_{{\Omega}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}_{N}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}}}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{p}}}{2}\int_{\Gamma}\bigl|\zeta_{N}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\omega_{N}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S+2\nu\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{f},N}(s)}\bigl|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N})\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\mu_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\mathrm{v}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\Omega}|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}_{N}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s+\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega_{N}^{\delta}}\bigl|\nabla p_{N}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s+\beta\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma(s)}\bigl|(\zeta_{N}^{*}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N})\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}t\leq E_{0}.}

Thus, exploiting the weak and weak* convergences collected in
Section 6
, noting that the weak convergence of the symmetrized gradient
\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{u}_{N})
is obtained without loss of integrability on the moving domain due to the uniform energy estimates derived in
Section 6
, and using the weak lower semicontinuity of the norms, we obtain the energy estimate stated in the second part of
Theorem 4.8
. This completes the proof of the main result of this work,
Theorem 4.8
.

9.
Conclusions

In this paper, we proved the existence of a finite-energy weak solution to a regularized 3D FSI problem between an incompressible, viscous fluid and a multilayered poro(visco)elastic structure. The structure is described by the Biot equations of poro(visco)elasticity, coupled with a thin reticular plate at the interface. The coupling between the fluid and the multilayered structure is nonlinear, which necessitates considering a moving interface. The resulting geometric nonlinearities led to considerable challenges in the existence proof. The proof is constructive, based on a splitting scheme that decouples the problem into a plate subproblem and a fluid-Biot subproblem. These subproblems are solved separately to construct approximate solutions. We then pass to the limit by establishing uniform bounds, deducing the corresponding weak and weak* convergences, and employing compactness arguments to obtain the strong convergence needed for the nonlinear terms. While the existence of a weak solution is shown for the poroelastic case, the poroviscoelastic case is also considered as it may be advantageous for future work on weak-classical consistency and for practical applications where dissipation is typically present in the poroelastic region.

The present work is the first that addresses a nonlinearly coupled FSI problem with a multilayered poro(visco)elastic structure in three dimensions. Compared to the study of the two-dimensional problem in
[
30
]
, we faced several additional difficulties. First, as discussed in
Subsection 3.1
, the finite-energy space for the plate displacement only yields Hölder continuity in space, so the fluid domain could only be regarded as the subgraph of a Hölder-continuous function. Consequently, we had to invoke the Lagrangian trace, and the solution and test spaces needed to be defined carefully in
Subsection 4.1
. The lack of Lipschitz regularity of the plate displacement required us to be cautious when switching between the fixed and moving domain configurations due to a potential loss of integrability or regularity. This also became apparent in the uniform boundedness of the approximate fluid velocities in
Section 6
, which only covers
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{W}^{1,p}(\Omega_{\mathrm{f}})^{3})
for
p\in(1,2)
and excludes the case
p=2
. This, in turn, had implications for the weak and weak* convergences in
Section 6
and required us to identify the weak limit of the sequence of gradients of the fluid velocities in
Subsection 8.1
. Another consequence of the lower regularity of the plate displacements is visible in the compactness proof for the family of fluid velocities, see
Subsection 7.5
. Here, an adjustment of the generalized Aubin–Lions compactness criterion, as initially presented in
[
45
]
and further extended in
[
30
]
, was necessary to accommodate the loss of regularity. Finally, the limit passage in
Subsection 8.4
called for significantly more care with regard to the lack of Lipschitz continuity of the plate displacements.

In
[
30
]
, a weak-classical consistency result was shown, i.e., it was proved in 2D that as the regularization parameter tends to zero, the weak solution to the regularized problem converges to a classical solution of the original problem, provided a classical solution exists. It is a natural question whether such a result carries over to the present 3D case. As already observed in
[
30
]
, the current regularization procedure based on an odd extension does not seem appropriate due to the critical convergence rate of the convolution kernel in three dimensions. However, in a forthcoming work, we plan to show that a weak-classical consistency result can still be obtained by using a different extension of the structure displacement.

"The mathematical framework for the diffuse interface method applied to coupled problems in fluid dynamics".

Appendix A

The generalized Aubin–Lions compactness criterion

In the following, we state the variant of the generalized Aubin–Lions compactness criterion from
[
45
]
tailored to our setting.
It is a consequence of
[
45
, Theorem 3.1]
together with
[
45
, Remark 3.1(2)]
and
[
30
, Theorem 8.3]
, see also the discussion in the proof of
[
49
, Corollary 4.4]
for the adjustments of the second inequality of Property (C1).
We will comment more on the assumptions below.

Lemma A.1
.

Consider Hilbert spaces
V
and
H
with
V\Subset H
, and let
V_{N}^{n}
and
Q_{N}^{n}
be (isomorphic to) Hilbert spaces with
(V_{N}^{n},Q_{N}^{n})\hookrightarrow V\times V
, where the embeddings are uniformly continuous with respect to
N
and
n
, and where additionally

V_{N}^{n}\Subset\overline{Q_{N}^{n}}^{H}\hookrightarrow(Q_{N}^{n})^{\prime}.

Moreover, let
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\in V_{N}^{n}
,
n=1,\dots,N
, and define
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}
via
\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}
by piecewise constant approximation as in (
6.1
).
Assume that
(\boldsymbol{u}_{N})\subset\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;H)
such that the following properties (A)–(C) are fulfilled:

(A)

There is a universal constant
C>0
so that

(A1)

\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq C
and

(A2)

\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;H)}\leq C
.

(B)

There exists a constant
C>0
independent of
n
and
N
, an exponent
p\in[1,2)
and a sequence of nonnegative numbers
\{a_{N}^{n}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
for each
N
, satisfying
(\Delta t)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}|a_{N}^{n}|^{2}\leq C
uniformly in
N
such that

\left\|P_{N}^{n}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\right\|_{(Q_{N}^{n})^{\prime}}\leq C\bigl(a_{N}^{n}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}\|_{V_{N}^{n}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}\bigr)^{p}

for all
n=0,1,\dots,N-1
.

(C)

The function spaces
Q_{N}^{n}
and
V_{N}^{n}
depend in a smooth way on time.
More precisely, we have the following.

(C1)

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, or equivalently, for each
\Delta t>0
, and for all
l\in\{1,\dots,N\}
as well as
n\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
, there exist a space
Q_{N}^{n,l}\subset V
and operators
J_{N,l,n}^{i}\colon Q_{N}^{n,l}\to Q_{N}^{n+i}
,
i=0,1,\dots,l
, so that
\|J_{N,l,n}^{i}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n+i}}\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n,l}}
for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in Q_{N}^{n,l}
.
Furthermore, for
C>0
independent of
N
,
n
and
l
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left(J_{N,l,n}^{j+1}\boldsymbol{v}-J_{N,l,n}^{j}\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+j+1}\right)_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C(\Delta t)\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n,l}}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+j+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+j+1}},

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0ptj\in\{0,\dots,l-1\},

\displaystyle\|J_{N,l,n}^{i}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C(l\Delta t)^{\frac{1}{4}}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{Q_{N}^{n,l}},

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0pti\in\{0,\dots,l\}.

(C2)

Set
V_{N}^{n,l}=\overline{Q_{N}^{n,l}}^{V}
.
There are functions
I_{N,l,n}^{i}\colon V_{N}^{n+i}\to V_{N}^{n,l}
,
i=0,1,\dots,l
, as well as a universal constant
C>0
with

\displaystyle\|I_{N,l,n}^{i}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V_{N}^{n,l}}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V_{N}^{n+i}},

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0pti\in\{0,\dots,l\},

\displaystyle\|I_{N,l,n}^{i}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H}

\displaystyle\leq g(l\Delta t)\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V_{N}^{n+i}},

\displaystyle\text{for}\kern 5.0pti\in\{0,\dots,l\},

where
g\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a universal, monotonically increasing function with
g(h)\to 0
as
h\to 0
.

(C3)

The following Ehrling property is valid:
For every
\delta>0
, there is a constant
C(\delta)>0
independent of
n
,
l
and
N
with

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H}\leq\delta\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V_{N}^{n,l}}+C(\delta)\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{(Q_{N}^{n,l})^{\prime}},\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0pt\boldsymbol{v}\in V_{N}^{n,l}.

Then the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}_{N})
is relatively compact in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;H)
.

A few remarks on the generalized Aubin–Lions compactness criterion in
Appendix A
are in order now.

Remark A.2
.

(1)

The relaxation of
V_{N}^{n}
and
Q_{N}^{n}
merely being isomorphic to Hilbert spaces does not affect the arguments in
[
45
, Section 3]
.

(2)

In
[
45
, Theorem 3.1]
, in (A), for the aforementioned time shift operator
\tau_{\Delta t}
, it is additionally assumed that
\|\tau_{\Delta t}\boldsymbol{u}_{N}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Delta t,T;H)}^{2}\leq C\Delta t
.
However, as pointed out in
[
45
, Remark 3.1(2)]
, this assumption is not necessary.

(3)

The result in
[
45
, Theorem 3.1]
is originally formulated for the following more specific property instead of (B) in our case:
There exists a universal constant
C>0
so that for the orthogonal projector
P_{N}^{n}
onto
\overline{Q_{N}^{n}}^{H}
, it holds that

\left\|P_{N}^{n}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n}}{\Delta t}\right\|_{(Q_{N}^{n})^{\prime}}\leq C\bigl(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{N}^{n+1}\|_{V_{N}^{n+1}}+1\bigr),\kern 5.0pt\text{for}\kern 5.0ptn=0,\dots,N-1.

It is shown in
[
30
, Theorem 8.3]
that the above condition can be replaced by (B) in
Appendix A
.

(4)

A close inspection of
[
45
, Section 3]
shows that the adjustments of the statements in (C1) still give rise to the desired estimates, see also the discussion in the proof of
[
49
, Corollary 4.4]
.
